🟡 PARTIAL

70. Driver Explanation

Module 3 may produce deterministic technical explanations.

Example:

Orders declined 15.2% during the investigation period and show a strong statistically significant association with the revenue movement.

It must not say:

Orders caused the revenue decline.

Status

🟢 POLICY IMPLEMENTED

71. Module 3 Should Not Generate Executive Narrative

The output:

Orders declined 15.2%.

is analytical.

The output:

Revenue weakened primarily because customer demand fell, with order volume contributing most of the decline.

belongs to Module 5.

Status

🟢 BOUNDARY DEFINED

72. Module 3 Should Not Generate Recommendations

Correct:

Stockouts
↓
candidate driver
↓
Module 4 validates
↓
Module 5 recommends action

Not:

Stockouts
↓
Immediately:
Increase inventory.

The current plan explicitly establishes this boundary.

Status

🟢 BOUNDARY DEFINED

   │

H1 Orders decline

Contribution: 32%

Temporal: moderate
Confidence: 76%

H3 Stockouts
Contribution: 12%
Association: moderate
Temporal: strong
Confidence: 69%

Module 4 then validates these candidates using evidence.

This directly demonstrates the Round 2 requirement for a multi-factor KPI movement with known/simulated underlying drivers.

117. What Is Actually Left for Module 3

This is the important practical conclusion.

You should NOT rewrite Module 3 from scratch.

The core engine is already there.

The remaining work is primarily:

        ↓

        ↓

        ↓

        ↓

Bottom line

The current Module 3 is much further along than Module 2's remaining work might suggest. The repository already has the core quantitative architecture, including exact decomposition/Shapley attribution, statistical association, temporal alignment, segment consistency, contradiction detection, hypothesis scoring, ambiguity handling and evidence-request generation.

The real Module 3 work remaining is to make that engine genuinely BusinessIntelligence.ai-grade:

The engine should accept any governed KPI from Module 1 — including KPIs originating from a user's uploaded CSV/XLSX/JSON dataset — and produce defensible, deterministic, dataset-isolated candidate explanations without hallucinating causes.

The strongest architectural rule to preserve is:

MODULE 1
"What is the number?"
        ↓
MODULE 2
"Is the movement meaningful?"
        ↓
MODULE 3
"What could explain it?"
        ↓
MODULE 4
"What evidence supports or contradicts it?"
        ↓
MODULE 5
"What is the defensible story and what should we do?"

That is also exactly the separation demanded by the BusinessIntelligence.ai Round 2 brief, including the explicit distinction between deterministic/statistical processing and LLM processing.

And one final practical point: do not start another giant rewrite of Module 3. The current implementation notes already consider the core Module 3 engine implemented. The next engineering effort should be verification, bug/hardening fixes, uploaded-data generalisation, and the M3 → M4 contract, while keeping the current mathematical architecture stable.





















TRACE — MODULE 4 UPDATED IMPLEMENTATION PLAN
Evidence Retrieval, RAG, Evidence Scoring & Contradiction Engine
BusinessIntelligence.ai Edition

Module: Module 4
Primary question:

"What evidence supports or contradicts the candidate explanation?"

Module 1:

What is the number?

Module 2:

Is the movement meaningful?

Module 3:

What could explain it?

Module 4:

What evidence supports or contradicts those explanations?

Module 5:

What is the defensible story and what should we do?

This is the intended architectural boundary in the current implementation plan.

1. Status Legend
Status	Meaning
🟢 IMPLEMENTED	Capability/file already exists in current repository
🟡 PARTIAL / VERIFY	Architecture exists but implementation needs hardening/integration
🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT	Required capability is missing or currently only simulated
🔵 DEPENDENCY	Primarily supplied by another module but M4 must integrate it
2. Current Module 4 Reality

The current repository contains:

trace-app/
└── src/
    ├── app/
    │   ├── api/
    │   │   └── evidence/
    │   │       └── route.ts
    │   │
    │   └── evidence-graph/
    │
    ├── components/
    │   └── EvidenceGraph.tsx
    │
    └── server/
        └── evidence/
            └── index.ts

The API currently receives:

kpi
month

computes the KPI and passes the numeric KPI value to searchEvidence().

The current evidence implementation then:

evidence.json
      ↓
randomVector()
      ↓
cosine similarity
      ↓
top-N

rather than performing semantic retrieval.

The Evidence Graph UI currently converts retrieved evidence into a simple sequence of nodes rather than representing actual hypothesis/evidence/source relationships.

Therefore:

The existing Module 4 is a placeholder shell, not a completed Evidence/RAG engine.

3. Module 4 Objective

Module 4 receives:

EvidenceRequest[]
+
Module 3 hypotheses
+
KPI context
+
structured business data
+
unstructured documents

and produces:

Relevant evidence
+
Evidence scores
+
Supporting evidence
+
Contradictory evidence
+
Neutral evidence
+
Evidence gaps
+
Updated confidence
+
Provenance
+
Evidence graph
+
Retrieval telemetry

The implementation plan explicitly defines this as the bridge between M1–M3 and M5/M6.

4. Core Architecture

The final architecture should be:

                  MODULE 3
             Driver / Hypothesis
                    Engine
                       │
                       │ EvidenceRequest[]
                       ▼
              ┌──────────────────┐
              │   QUERY BUILDER  │
              └────────┬─────────┘
                       │
          ┌────────────┴─────────────┐
          ▼                          ▼
 STRUCTURED RETRIEVAL          DOCUMENT RETRIEVAL
          │                          │

          │                    ▼           ▼

          │                    │           │

          │                       HYBRID
          │                       MERGE
          │                          │

                     RERANKING
                         │

                    TOP-K EVIDENCE

This is the architecture explicitly defined in the latest plan.

5. Critical Architectural Rule

The pipeline must be:

Retrieval
    ↓
Relevance
    ↓
Evidence
    ↓
Support / Contradiction
    ↓
Confidence

NOT:

Retrieve text
    ↓
LLM says "this proves the hypothesis"

The implementation plan explicitly makes this distinction.

6. LLM Role

The LLM may assist with:

✓ semantic query expansion
✓ document interpretation
✓ evidence classification where deterministic rules are insufficient
✓ contextual understanding
✓ eventual narrative synthesis

The LLM must not be responsible for:

❌ KPI calculation
❌ quantitative truth
❌ contribution percentages
❌ source freshness
❌ mathematical evidence scores
❌ deciding that evidence "proves" a hypothesis without structured support

The Round 2 brief explicitly says teams must distinguish deterministic logic, SQL, statistics, retrieval and LLM processing.

7. RAG Is Only One Part of Module 4

Do not define:

Module 4 = RAG

Instead:

Module 4 =
Structured Retrieval
+
Keyword Retrieval
+
Vector Retrieval
+
Hybrid Retrieval
+
Reranking
+
Evidence Scoring
+
Contradiction Detection
+
Confidence Calibration
+
Provenance
+
Evidence Graph

The current implementation plan explicitly locks this architecture.

8. Primary Input — EvidenceRequest

Module 3 already produces the critical interface:

interface EvidenceRequest {
  hypothesisId: string

  metric: string
  period: string

  driver: string

  query: string

  filters: {
    region?: string
    product?: string
    channel?: string

    dateStart?: string
    dateEnd?: string

    [key: string]: unknown
  }

  requiredEvidence: string[]
}

The latest plan explicitly identifies EvidenceRequest[] as the primary Module 4 input.

Status

🟢 IMPLEMENTED IN MODULE 3 CONTRACT

Required

🟡 VERIFY M4 consumes the complete contract

9. Do Not Replace EvidenceRequest With Free-Form Prompt

Bad:

POST /evidence

{
  "prompt": "find why revenue declined"
}

Good:

EvidenceRequest
{
  hypothesisId: "H1",
  metric: "revenue",
  period: "2026-08",
  driver: "orders",
  query: "orders decline North August 2026",
  filters: {
    region: "North",
    dateStart: "2026-08-01",
    dateEnd: "2026-08-31"
  }
}

This makes retrieval:

deterministic
traceable
auditable
10. Query Builder

Create:

src/server/evidence/retrieval/queryBuilder.ts

or equivalent.

Input:

EvidenceRequest

Output:

RetrievalQuery

Example:

{
  "textQuery": "orders decline North August 2026",
  "metric": "orders",
  "driver": "orders",
  "region": "North",
  "dateStart": "2026-08-01",
  "dateEnd": "2026-08-31",
  "requiredEvidence": [
    "sales"
  ]
}

The plan explicitly defines metadata extraction from:

metric
driver
region
product
date
required evidence

before retrieval.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

11. Metadata Filtering

Metadata must be applied before or during retrieval.

For:

North Product A stockouts August 2026

do not return:

South Product A July 2026

merely because the wording is semantically similar.

The implementation plan explicitly calls out this failure mode.

Required filters
region
product
channel
metric
driver
date
dateStart
dateEnd
sourceType
documentType
Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

12. Structured Evidence Retrieval

This is extremely important.

Not every piece of evidence should come from documents.

For quantitative claims:

Module 1 database
        ↓
SQL
        ↓
structured evidence

Example:

Orders

July:
28,817

August:
24,424

Change:
−15.24%

The implementation plan explicitly defines structured business data as a first-class evidence source.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

13. Structured Evidence Sources

Initially support:

sales_transactions
marketing_daily
operations_daily

and governed KPI definitions.

Example:

Orders
→ sales_transactions

Conversion
→ marketing_daily

Stockouts
→ operations_daily

Module 4 must query these rather than duplicating their data.

14. Structured Evidence Must Be Deterministic

Example:

SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT order_id)
FROM sales_transactions
WHERE date BETWEEN ? AND ?
AND region = ?

Then calculate:

current
previous
change

with deterministic code.

No LLM.

15. Critical Module Boundary

Module 1:

Orders decreased 15.24%.

Module 3:

Orders could explain the revenue decrease.

Module 4:

Governed sales data supports the Orders hypothesis.

Module 5:

Revenue declined primarily alongside a substantial decline in Orders, supported by the available evidence.

This distinction is explicitly established in the implementation plan.

16. Document Corpus

Module 4 also needs an unstructured evidence corpus.

Example documents:

Operations Report — August
Marketing Campaign Report — August
Inventory Incident Report
Customer Support Summary
Regional Performance Memo
Supply Chain Update
Pricing Review
Campaign Performance Report

The prototype does not need real confidential enterprise documents.

Synthetic documents are acceptable.

The Round 2 brief explicitly allows simulated/sample data.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT PROPERLY

17. Synthetic Evidence Corpus

The synthetic corpus should contain both:

supporting documents

and:

contradictory documents

Example:

Supporting

North inventory availability fell below target for six days in August.

Contradictory

North inventory availability remained above target throughout August.

The second should deliberately create a contradiction scenario.

Add:

evidence_documents

19. Evidence Chunks Table

Add:

evidence_chunks

Fields:

id

text

end_offset

region
product

date_start
date_end

metadata

Chunking allows retrieval at paragraph/section level instead of returning entire documents.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

20. Chunking Strategy

Do not arbitrarily split documents into tiny fragments.

Prefer:

heading
+
paragraphs
+
table context

where possible.

Each chunk must retain enough context to make the evidence understandable independently.

21. Content Hash

Each document/chunk should have:

content_hash

This provides:

deduplication
embedding cache invalidation
document version tracking
Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

22. Source Types

Support:

structured
document
report
policy
incident
support_ticket
external

For the hackathon:

structured
+
internal_document

are enough for the core vertical slice.

23. Source Quality

Every source should have:

sourceQuality

Example:

1.0 → governed official source
0.9 → approved internal report
0.7 → analyst note
0.5 → low-confidence source

Do not treat all sources equally.

The implementation plan explicitly requires source-quality scoring.

24. Source Freshness

Evidence must contain:

documentDate
retrievedAt
lastUpdatedAt

and ideally:

freshnessScore

Example:

August 30 report
retrieved August 31

should be stronger for an August investigation than:

January report

The Round 2 prototype explicitly requires evidence showing source freshness.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

25. Freshness Must Not Override Relevance

A very recent irrelevant document should not outrank:

an older but directly relevant incident report

Therefore:

final score
=
relevance
+
freshness
+
source quality
+
temporal alignment
...

not freshness alone.

26. Keyword Retrieval

Implement deterministic keyword retrieval.

Possible approach:

tokenisation
+
normalisation
+
term matching
+
BM25-like scoring

For the SQLite prototype, a lightweight implementation is sufficient.

Potentially use SQLite FTS where practical.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

27. Vector Retrieval

Implement real embeddings.

Current:

randomVector()

must be removed.

The current implementation literally generates random 128-dimensional vectors and caches them in memory.

That is not semantic retrieval.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

28. Real Embeddings

The embedding pipeline should be:

Document
   ↓
Chunk
   ↓
Embedding model
   ↓
Vector
   ↓
Persist

Query:

EvidenceRequest
   ↓
Query embedding
   ↓
Vector similarity
   ↓
Candidate evidence
29. Embedding Persistence

Add:

evidence_embeddings

Fields:

created_at

The implementation plan explicitly requires embeddings to be persisted and reused.

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

30. Embedding Cache

Do not recompute:

document embedding

on every request.

Cache based on:

content_hash
+
embedding_model

Example:

same document
same model
→ reuse embedding
Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

31. Query Embedding Cache

Also consider caching frequent query embeddings.

Cache key:

normalisedQuery
+
embeddingModel

This is Tier 2 rather than absolutely necessary for the first demo.

Status

🟡 OPTIONAL / TIER 2

32. Hybrid Retrieval

This is the core of Module 4.

Run:

Keyword search
       +
Vector search
       +
Structured search

then merge.

The implementation plan explicitly rejects pure vector RAG.

33. Why Hybrid?

Suppose query:

North Product A stockouts August 2026

Keyword search is good at:

North
Product A
August
stockouts

Vector search is good at:

availability failure
inventory shortage
product unavailable

Structured search is good at:

stockouts = 18

Hybrid retrieval combines all three.

34. Retrieval Candidate Pool

Example:

Structured:
top 10

Keyword:
top 20

Vector:
top 20

Then:

union
↓
deduplicate
↓
rerank
↓
top 5–10

Exact numbers should be configurable.

35. Hybrid Merge

Possible normalised score:

hybridScore =
    α × keywordScore
  + β × vectorScore
  + γ × structuredScore

with configurable:

α + β + γ = 1

Example:

keyword = 0.25
vector = 0.35
structured = 0.40

Do not hard-code these throughout the codebase.

36. Reranking

After hybrid retrieval:

candidate evidence
       ↓
reranker
       ↓
final relevance

Reranking should consider:

semantic relevance
metadata match
temporal relevance
entity match
hypothesis alignment
source quality

The implementation plan explicitly requires reranking after hybrid retrieval.

37. Reranker Does Not Need an LLM Initially

For the hackathon, deterministic reranking is preferable:

metadata match
+
keyword match
+
vector similarity
+
date relevance
+
driver relevance
+
source quality

This keeps:

cost low
latency low
behavior explainable

A cross-encoder/LLM reranker can be a future enhancement.

38. Evidence Relevance Score

Each evidence item should expose:

relevanceScore

Example:

0.91

This is distinct from:

supportScore

and:

finalEvidenceScore
39. Evidence Scoring

The plan requires multiple dimensions.

Recommended:

EvidenceScore =
    w1 × relevance
  + w2 × sourceQuality
  + w3 × temporalRelevance
  + w4 × entityMatch
  + w5 × directionMatch
  + w6 × hypothesisAlignment

All weights should be centralised.

40. Source Quality Score

Example:

Governed database:
1.00

Official operations report:
0.95

Approved internal analyst report:
0.85

Unverified note:
0.50

These are illustrative and configurable.

41. Temporal Relevance

Example:

Hypothesis:

August revenue decline

Evidence:

August 15 stockout report

→ high temporal relevance.

Evidence:

January stockout report

→ low temporal relevance.

42. Entity Match

Check:

region
product
channel
campaign

Example:

Hypothesis:
North Product A

Evidence:
North Product A

strong.

Evidence:
South Product B

weak or irrelevant.

43. Direction Match

Hypothesis:

Stockouts ↑
Revenue ↓

Evidence:

Stockouts increased

→ direction supports hypothesis.

Evidence:

Stockouts decreased

→ contradiction.

44. Hypothesis Alignment

Evidence should explicitly relate to:

driver
metric
period
scope

Example:

Driver:
stockouts

Evidence:
inventory shortage caused 18 stockout events

→ high alignment.

45. Evidence Classification

Every retrieved item should be classified:

SUPPORT
CONTRADICT
NEUTRAL

The architecture explicitly requires this.

46. Support Evidence

Example:

Hypothesis:
Orders decline explains revenue decline

Evidence:
Orders decreased 15.24% in August.

Classification:

SUPPORT
47. Contradictory Evidence

Example:

Hypothesis:
Stockouts explain revenue decline

Evidence:
Stockout incidents decreased by 12%.

Classification:

CONTRADICT
48. Neutral Evidence

Example:

Evidence:
The company launched a new employee benefits policy in August.

If unrelated to revenue:

NEUTRAL

Do not force every retrieved result into support/contradiction.

49. Contradiction Engine

Create:

src/server/evidence/contradiction.ts

Responsibilities:

detect direction mismatch
detect entity mismatch
detect temporal mismatch
detect conflicting claims
detect conflicting structured evidence
Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

50. Ambiguous Evidence

If:

support ≈ contradict

do not arbitrarily select one.

Return:

status = mixed

The implementation plan explicitly requires this.

51. Contradiction Strength

A contradiction should have:

strength

such as:

weak
moderate
strong

Example:

weak contradiction:
one low-quality analyst note

strong contradiction:
governed operations data
52. Evidence Independence

Two documents copied from the same report are not:

2 independent pieces of evidence

They may actually represent:

1 source

Therefore track:

sourceId
documentId

and avoid double-counting.

The implementation plan places evidence independence in Tier 2.

Status

🟡 TIER 2

53. Evidence Diversity

Prefer:

sales data
+
operations report
+
support tickets

over:

three copies of the same report

This makes the evidence package more robust.

Status

🟡 TIER 2

54. Evidence Gaps

If the hypothesis requires:

competition data

but no such evidence exists:

evidenceGap:
competition pricing data unavailable

Do not silently ignore it.

55. EvidenceGap Structure

Example:

interface EvidenceGap {
  hypothesisId: string
  missingEvidence: string
  reason: string
  severity: "low" | "medium" | "high"
}
Status

🟡 PART OF TARGET CONTRACT

56. Confidence Update

Module 3 gives:

priorConfidence

Module 4 calculates:

evidenceConfidence

then:

updatedConfidence

The architecture is:

M3 confidence
      ↓
Evidence
      ↓
Evidence quality
      ↓
Support / contradiction
      ↓
M4 updated confidence

This is explicitly required.

57. Do Not Let Evidence Completely Override Analytics

If Module 3 says:

Orders confidence = 0.90

and one weak document says:

Orders are stable

Module 4 should not suddenly produce:

confidence = 0.10

Evidence weight should depend on:

source quality
relevance
independence
recency
support strength
contradiction strength
58. Confidence Formula

A simple configurable formulation:

evidenceConfidence =
supportStrength
− contradictionStrength

normalised to:

0–1

Then:

updatedConfidence =
α × priorConfidence
+
(1 − α) × evidenceConfidence

with:

α

configurable.

A more sophisticated calibration can be introduced later.

59. Confidence Must Be Bounded

Always:

0 ≤ confidence ≤ 1

Never:

confidence = 1.23
confidence = -0.4
60. Evidence Status

The final evidence state should be:

supported
mixed
insufficient_evidence

Exactly as defined by the target contract.

61. Abstention

This is critical for Round 2.

If:

retrievalScore < threshold

or:

evidenceCount < minimum

or:

support ≈ contradiction

then:

insufficient_evidence

or:

mixed

instead of inventing certainty.

62. Low-Confidence Scenario

Example:

Revenue ↓ 12%

Hypothesis:
Competition caused decline

Available evidence:
none

Confidence:
Module 3 = 0.42

Module 4:
insufficient evidence

Return:

status:
insufficient_evidence

and:

evidenceGaps:
[
  "competitor pricing",
  "competitor promotions",
  "market-share data"
]

This directly satisfies a Round 2 minimum prototype expectation.

63. Clarification Request

Where useful:

Additional evidence required:
competitor pricing data for August

This is better than hallucinating a conclusion.

64. Provenance

Every evidence item must answer:

Where did this come from?

Minimum:

interface Provenance {
  sourceId: string
  documentId?: string
  chunkId?: string

  sourceType: string

  uri?: string

  retrievedAt: string

  model?: string
  promptVersion?: string
}

This exact structure is defined in the current plan.

65. Provenance Chain

The final system should allow:

Narrative statement
        ↓
Hypothesis
        ↓
Evidence
        ↓
Chunk
        ↓
Document
        ↓
Source

This is one of TRACE's strongest differentiators.

66. Evidence Citation

Every evidence item shown to the user should expose something like:

Operations Report — August
Source: Operations System
Date: 2026-08-15
Region: North
Evidence score: 0.91
Supports: Stockouts hypothesis

The user should be able to inspect the source.

67. Source URI

For documents:

uri

can point to:

local document
internal document ID
uploaded file
approved external source

For structured data:

source:
sales_transactions

is sufficient initially.

68. Uploaded User Documents

This is an important BusinessIntelligence.ai generalisation.

If the user uploads:

inventory_report_august.pdf

Module 4 should eventually support:

upload
↓
parse
↓
extract metadata
↓
chunk
↓
embed
↓
persist
↓
retrieve

Structured upload
CSV

JSON

should primarily feed:

Module 1
Unstructured upload

TXT
reports
memos

should primarily feed:

Module 4

This preserves the architectural boundary.

70. Dataset Isolation

Every evidence request should eventually carry:

datasetId
datasetVersionId

This is especially important for user-uploaded data.

Example:

Company A
Dataset v4

must not retrieve evidence from:

Company A
Dataset v2

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

71. Analysis ID

This should be locked as the cross-module correlation key:

analysisId

The implementation plan explicitly recommends it across Modules 1–6.

Flow:

analysisId
│
├── Module 1
├── Module 2
├── Module 3
├── Module 4
├── Module 5
├── Decision
├── Evidence Graph
├── Telemetry
└── Audit

🟡 ARCHITECTURE DEFINED / IMPLEMENTATION REQUIRED

72. Evidence Graph

The graph should represent:

     │
     ▼

     │
     ▼

     │
 ┌───┴────┐
 ▼        ▼
Evidence  Contradiction
 │
 ▼
Document
 │
 ▼
Source

The plan explicitly defines these relationships.

73. Graph Relationship Types

Support:

SUPPORTS
CONTRADICTS
DERIVED_FROM
RETRIEVED_FOR
RELATES_TO

Example:

H1 Orders
    │
    ├── SUPPORTS → Orders August report
    │
    └── CONTRADICTS → Sales stability report
74. Current Evidence Graph Problem

The current EvidenceGraph.tsx creates nodes from returned evidence and connects them sequentially:

Evidence 1 → Evidence 2 → Evidence 3

That is not a real evidence graph.

The graph must instead represent actual semantic relationships:

Revenue
 ↓
Orders Hypothesis
 ↓
Orders −15.24%
 ↓
Sales Data

and:

Revenue
 ↓
Stockouts Hypothesis
 ↓
Stockout Report
 ↓
Operations System
Status

🔴 REBUILD GRAPH LOGIC

75. Evidence Graph Persistence

Persist graph relations in:

evidence_relations

Recommended fields:

Status

🔴 YET TO IMPLEMENT

76. EvidencePackage

The stable output of Module 4 should be:

interface EvidencePackage {
  analysisId: string

  metric: string
  period: string

  hypotheses: EvidenceHypothesis[]

  evidence: EvidenceItem[]

  contradictions: EvidenceContradiction[]

  evidenceGaps: EvidenceGap[]

  overallConfidence: number

  status:
    | "supported"
    | "mixed"
    | "insufficient_evidence"

  provenance: Provenance[]

  telemetry: RetrievalTelemetry
}

This is the target contract explicitly defined in the current implementation plan.

77. EvidenceHypothesis

For each hypothesis:

interface EvidenceHypothesis {
  hypothesisId: string
  driver: string

  priorConfidence: number

  evidenceConfidence: number

  updatedConfidence: number

  supportingEvidence: string[]
  contradictoryEvidence: string[]
  neutralEvidence: string[]

  evidenceCount: number

  status:
    | "supported"
    | "mixed"
    | "insufficient_evidence"
}

This is already the intended stable structure.

78. Evidence Item

Recommended structure:

interface EvidenceItem {
  id: string

  hypothesisId?: string

  text: string

  sourceType: string
  sourceId: string

  documentId?: string
  chunkId?: string

  metric?: string
  driver?: string

  region?: string
  product?: string
  channel?: string

  date?: string
  dateStart?: string
  dateEnd?: string

  relevanceScore: number
  evidenceScore: number

  classification:
    | "support"
    | "contradict"
    | "neutral"

  sourceQuality: number
  temporalRelevance: number

  provenance: Provenance
}
79. Retrieval Telemetry

Round 2 explicitly requires runtime telemetry covering:

latency
model calls
token usage
estimated cost

Module 4 should record:

retrievalLatencyMs
keywordLatencyMs
vectorLatencyMs
structuredLatencyMs
rerankLatencyMs
scoringLatencyMs

candidateCount
finalEvidenceCount

embeddingCalls
embeddingTokens
embeddingCost

llmCalls
llmTokens
llmCost

cacheHit
80. LLM Economics

If Module 4 uses an embedding model or LLM:

track:

model
tokens
latency
estimated cost

This becomes part of the audit story.

81. Embedding Provider Abstraction

Do not hard-code one provider throughout the codebase.

Use:

interface EmbeddingProvider {
  embedText(text: string): Promise<number[]>
  embedBatch(texts: string[]): Promise<number[][]>
  modelName(): string
  dimension(): number
}

Then support:

local model
OpenAI-compatible provider
other provider

without rewriting retrieval.

82. No Provider Credentials in Code

Use:

environment variables

and:

provider configuration

Never commit API keys.

83. Local/Offline Fallback

For hackathon reliability, a local embedding option is valuable.

If the embedding provider is unavailable:

Vector retrieval unavailable
        ↓
Keyword + structured retrieval
        ↓
Evidence still works

Do not make the whole application fail because embeddings are unavailable.

84. Graceful Degradation

Recommended fallback:

Vector available?
   │
 YES → Hybrid
 NO
   ↓
Keyword + Structured
   ↓
EvidencePackage

But the package should clearly state:

vector retrieval unavailable

in telemetry/caveats.

85. Retrieval Failure

If retrieval fails:

Do not return empty success.

Return:

status:
insufficient_evidence

plus:

retrievalError

in telemetry/internal logs.

86. No Evidence Scenario

If:

candidate = competition

but:

documents = 0
structured data = 0

return:

evidenceCount = 0
status = insufficient_evidence

not:

competition caused revenue decline
87. Sparse Evidence Scenario

If only:

1 low-quality source

exists:

confidence = low
status = insufficient_evidence

or potentially:

mixed

depending on contradiction.

88. Contradiction Scenario

Suppose:

Source A:
Stockouts increased 35%.

Source B:
Stockouts decreased 20%.

Then:

supportingEvidence = [A]
contradictoryEvidence = [B]

status = mixed

and:

updatedConfidence

must be reduced accordingly.

89. Evidence Aggregation

Do not simply count documents.

Bad:

5 documents = high confidence

Better:

support quality
+
independence
+
source quality
+
relevance
+
temporal alignment
+
contradictions
90. Evidence Confidence

Example:

3 strong supporting sources
0 contradictions

→ high.

1 weak supporting source
2 neutral sources

→ low.

2 strong support
2 strong contradiction

→ mixed.

91. Overall Confidence

Aggregate hypothesis confidence carefully.

For example:

H1 Orders       0.88
H2 AOV          0.72
H3 Stockouts    0.51

Overall package confidence should reflect:

quality of evidence for leading hypotheses

rather than simple arithmetic average.

92. Evidence Ranking

Final evidence returned to Module 5 should be:

Top supporting evidence
+
Top contradictory evidence
+
important neutral evidence

Do not send hundreds of chunks to Module 5.

93. Top-K Policy

Recommended:

Top 5 supporting
Top 3 contradictory
Top 2 neutral

for the initial prototype.

Make configurable.

94. Evidence Deduplication

Two nearly identical chunks should not appear as:

Evidence 1
Evidence 2
Evidence 3
Evidence 4

if they are all copies of the same statement.

Deduplicate using:

content hash
document ID
source ID
similarity
95. Evidence Diversity

When possible:

structured
+
operations report
+
customer support

should outrank:

three identical operations reports

This is particularly useful for the judge-facing evidence graph.

96. Module 4 API

The current endpoint:

GET /api/evidence?kpi=revenue&month=2026-08

is too weak because it ignores Module 3 hypotheses.

The target should support something conceptually like:

GET /api/evidence
    ?analysisId=...
    &metric=revenue
    &period=2026-08

or preferably:

POST /api/evidence/retrieve

with:

{
  "analysisId": "...",
  "requests": [...]
}
Status

🟡 CURRENT API EXISTS / REWORK REQUIRED

EvidenceRequest[] can become complex.

A POST body can carry:

multiple hypotheses
filters
required evidence
dataset context

without putting everything into a URL.

      ↓

      ↓

2 days old

or:

Operations System

108. Analytical Method UI

SQL aggregation

sales_transactions

COUNT(DISTINCT order_id)

For vector evidence:

Method:
semantic retrieval

<model>

This demonstrates the required LLM/non-LLM separation.

109. LLM vs Non-LLM Breakdown

The demo should explicitly show:

MODULE 4 PROCESSING

Structured retrieval       → SQL
Keyword retrieval          → deterministic
Vector retrieval           → embedding model
Hybrid merge               → deterministic
Reranking                  → deterministic
Evidence scoring           → deterministic
Contradiction rules        → deterministic
Confidence calibration     → deterministic

If LLM is used:

Semantic query expansion   → LLM

and clearly label it.

110. Security

Module 4 must inherit upstream access controls.

If the user cannot access:

North region

Module 4 must not retrieve:

North evidence

even if the query requests it.

111. Row-Level Security

Example:

Regional analyst:
North only

Retrieval filter must enforce:

region = North

before:

keyword/vector ranking

not after retrieval.

This prevents restricted evidence from entering the candidate pool.

112. Column-Level Security

Sensitive fields should not enter:

embedding

or:

LLM prompt

if the user is not authorised to access them.

This is important for enterprise RAG.

113. Domain-Level Security

A user may have:

Sales domain ✓
Operations domain ✓
HR domain ✗

Module 4 must not retrieve HR documents simply because they are semantically relevant.

114. Auditability

For every evidence retrieval:

analysisId
user/role
query
filters
sources considered
sources selected
scores
classification
timestamp
model

should eventually be auditable.

This feeds Module 6.

115. Source Freshness + Lineage

Every evidence package should make it possible to answer:

Where?
When?
Which source?
Which version?
Which chunk?
Which retrieval method?
Which model?
Which score?

That is the TRACE value proposition.

116. Uploaded Document Versioning

If:

inventory_report.pdf

is uploaded again with new content:

v2

do not overwrite provenance for:

v1

Use:

content_hash
+
document version
117. Data Drift

Module 4 should eventually monitor:

document distribution changes
source freshness changes
retrieval relevance degradation
embedding model changes

For hackathon:

telemetry + evaluation

is sufficient.

118. Retrieval Evaluation

Create known queries:

Q1:
North stockouts August

Q2:
Orders decline August

Q3:
AOV decline North

Q4:
Marketing conversion decline

Each should have known relevant documents.

Measure:

Recall@K
Precision@K
MRR
119. Evidence Classification Evaluation

Known ground truth:

Document A → support
Document B → contradict
Document C → neutral

Test whether the classifier gets the labels right.

120. Ground Truth Evidence Scenario

Example:

H1:
Stockouts contributed to revenue decline

Ground truth:
Support:
Operations Report A
Operations Report B

Contradict:
Operations Report C

Neutral:
Marketing Report D

Module 4 should reproduce this structure.

122. Multi-Factor Evidence

Round 2 specifically requires a multi-factor KPI movement with known/simulated drivers.

├── Orders

[ ] graph construction
[ ] uploaded document
148. Retrieval Tests

Example:

Query:
North stockouts August

Expected:
North August stockout report

Must not rank:

South July stockout report

above it simply because the text is similar.

149. Contradiction Tests
Hypothesis:
Stockouts increased

Evidence:
Stockouts decreased

Expected:
CONTRADICT
150. Temporal Tests
Hypothesis period:
August

Evidence:
January

Expected:
low temporal score
151. Entity Tests
Hypothesis:
North

Evidence:
South

Expected:
entity mismatch
152. Source Quality Tests
Governed DB > official report > analyst note

when other factors are equal.

153. Abstention Tests
No evidence

Expected:

insufficient_evidence

not:

supported
154. Mixed Evidence Tests
Support = 0.80
Contradiction = 0.78

Expected:

mixed

rather than:

supported
155. Confidence Tests

Input:

priorConfidence = 0.80
strong support

Expected:

updatedConfidence > priorConfidence

For:

strong contradiction

expected:

updatedConfidence < priorConfidence
156. Determinism

Same:

dataset
query
documents
configuration
model

should produce the same retrieval/ranking results, subject to deterministic provider behavior.

157. Performance

Measure:

structured retrieval
keyword retrieval
vector retrieval
hybrid merge
reranking
scoring
total

Target for the prototype:

sub-second to a few seconds

depending on embedding provider.

The exact target should be measured rather than fabricated.

158. Caching

Cache:

document embeddings
query embeddings
retrieval results

where safe.

Cache key must include:

dataset/version
query
filters
embedding model
configuration version
159. Security-Correct Caching

Never cache:

North evidence

and then serve it to:

South-only analyst

Therefore access scope must participate in the cache key.

160. Observability

Log:

analysisId
hypothesisId
query
retrieval methods
candidate count
selected count
scores
latency
model
cache

Do not log sensitive document contents unnecessarily.

161. Privacy

For uploaded enterprise documents:

PII
credentials
customer data
sensitive fields

must not automatically be embedded or exposed.

Apply access control before indexing/retrieval.

162. Document Access Control

At ingestion:

document
 ↓
security metadata
 ↓
embedding

At retrieval:

query
 ↓
authorized candidate filter
 ↓
retrieval

This is safer than retrieving everything and filtering afterward.

163. External Evidence

The architecture can eventually support:

external news
market reports
competition data

but for the hackathon:

synthetic/internal evidence

is enough.

The Round 2 brief explicitly allows reasonable assumptions and simulated data.

164. Evidence Freshness Hierarchy

Potential ranking:

real-time governed data
>
recent official report
>
recent analyst report
>
old report

but relevance must still dominate.

165. Evidence Quality vs Evidence Relevance

Do not confuse:

high-quality source

with:

relevant evidence

Example:

Official annual report

may be authoritative but irrelevant to:

August stockouts
166. Quantitative Evidence Priority

For numerical claims:

structured governed data

should generally outrank:

LLM-extracted number from a document

because Module 1 is the quantitative source of truth.

167. Document Numbers

If a document says:

Orders declined 15%.

and Module 1 says:

Orders declined 15.24%

Module 4 should prefer:

15.24%

from structured data for quantitative truth, while the document can provide contextual support.

168. Evidence Reconciliation

When structured and unstructured evidence disagree:

Structured:
stockouts +35%

Document:
stockouts +10%

do not silently choose one.

Return:

mixed evidence

or flag the discrepancy for investigation.

169. Evidence Contradiction vs Data Quality Issue

Sometimes disagreement is not a true contradiction.

Example:

Structured:
August monthly stockouts +35%

Document:
first week stockouts +10%

These can both be true.

Therefore temporal scope must be considered before marking contradiction.

170. Evidence Scope

Each evidence item should carry:

scope

such as:

monthly
weekly
regional
product
company-wide

This helps avoid false contradictions.

171. Entity Resolution

Eventually support aliases:

"Product A"
"P-A"
"SKU001"

mapping to the same governed entity.

This can initially be handled by Module 1 semantic metadata.

172. Query Expansion

Optional LLM-assisted query expansion:

stockouts
→
inventory shortage
→
product unavailable
→
availability issue

But:

original query

must remain traceable.

173. Query Expansion Must Not Change Scope

If query says:

North

LLM must not expand it to:

all regions

Metadata constraints remain authoritative.

174. Retrieval Pipeline With LLM

If used:

EvidenceRequest
       ↓
Deterministic metadata
       ↓
LLM query expansion
       ↓
Keyword/vector retrieval
       ↓
Deterministic filtering

not:

LLM decides what evidence to retrieve

without constraints.

184. Final TRACE Architecture After Module 4

Once M4 is complete, the system becomes:

┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        TRACE                               │
│                                                            │
│  USER DATA                                                 │

│     │                                                      │

│     │                                                      │
│     ▼                                                      │

│     │                                                      │
│     ▼                                                      │

│  Evidence / RAG                                             │
│     │                                                      │
│     ├── Structured evidence                                │
│     ├── Keyword retrieval                                  │
│     ├── Vector retrieval                                   │

│     │                                                      │
│     ▼                                                      │

│  Governance / Audit / Feedback                              │
│                                                            │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is the coherent vertical slice the Round 2 BusinessIntelligence.ai requirements are asking for: detect → reconcile → explain → substantiate → communicate uncertainty → recommend → learn → govern.

Bottom line

Module 4 is the biggest remaining technical gap in TRACE right now. The repository has the shell, but the current implementation is still using random embeddings and a numeric KPI pseudo-vector, so that part cannot be presented as genuine RAG/evidence intelligence.

The correct next milestone is therefore:

Replace the fake evidence search with a small, real, hybrid evidence engine — structured SQL + keyword + real embeddings + metadata filtering + reranking + evidence scoring + contradiction + provenance — while keeping M1–M3 frozen.

That gives you the strongest possible M3 → M4 → M5 vertical slice without destabilising the work already completed. The latest plan explicitly recommends exactly this architecture.

And the Round 2 requirement document should remain the external acceptance criterion: in particular, the prototype needs 3–5 connected KPIs, 2–3 heterogeneous sources, a semantic contract, two personas, a multi-factor movement, low-confidence abstention, sparse history, role-based security, freshness/method/contribution/confidence/lineage evidence, LLM-vs-non-LLM transparency, and runtime telemetry.
























TRACE — MODULE 5 UPDATED IMPLEMENTATION PLAN
Intelligence, Storytelling, Recommendation & Decision Workspace

Module: Module 5

Primary question:

"What is the defensible story, what should we do, and how should the decision be recorded?"

The TRACE chain is now:

MODULE 1
"What is the number?"
        ↓
MODULE 2
"Is the movement meaningful?"
        ↓
MODULE 3
"What could explain it?"
        ↓
MODULE 4
"What evidence supports or contradicts it?"
        ↓
MODULE 5
"What is the defensible story?"
        ↓
DECISION
"What should we do?"
        ↓
MODULE 6
"Can we prove how TRACE reached this decision?"

This boundary is explicitly established in the current implementation plan.

1. Current Module 5 Status

The latest implementation plan estimates:

🟡 ~0–10% implemented

The architecture is substantially defined, but actual implementation should begin after the Module 4 vertical slice is stable.

So unlike M1–M3, Module 5 should currently be treated primarily as:

Architecture:
🟢 Defined

Contracts:
🟡 Defined, finalisation required

Implementation:
🔴 Mostly pending
2. What Module 5 Actually Is

Module 5 converts:

KPI truth
+
Signal truth
+
Driver truth
+
Evidence truth
+
Business context
+
User persona

into:

Business intelligence
+
Narrative
+
Recommendation
+
Decision workspace
+
Human feedback
+
Decision memory

The latest plan explicitly describes this as the Intelligence and Decision layer of TRACE.

3. What Module 5 Owns

Module 5 owns:

1. KPI storytelling
2. Executive summary
3. Driver explanation
4. Evidence presentation
5. Uncertainty communication
6. Alternative explanations
7. Persona adaptation
8. Narrative generation
9. Recommendation generation
10. Action prioritisation
11. Expected-impact framing
12. Owner assignment

16. Decision logging
17. Analyst feedback
18. Decision memory
19. Narrative versioning
20. LLM telemetry
21. LLM cost tracking
22. Final Decision Workspace

This responsibility boundary is explicitly defined in the latest plan.

4. What Module 5 Must NOT Own

Module 5 must not perform:

❌ KPI calculation
❌ anomaly detection
❌ contribution analysis
❌ statistical root-cause analysis
❌ vector retrieval
❌ raw document ingestion
❌ evidence retrieval
❌ quantitative truth generation

Those belong upstream.

Specifically:

M1 → KPI truth
M2 → signal truth
M3 → driver/hypothesis truth
M4 → evidence truth
M5 → interpretation + communication + action

This separation is explicitly locked in the implementation plan.

This ensures that an API-key failure, model outage, token limit, or provider failure cannot break TRACE.

8. IntelligenceContext

Create:

src/server/story/context.ts

The purpose is to combine M1–M4 into one immutable context object.

Conceptually:

interface IntelligenceContext {
  analysisId: string

  metric: string
  period: string

  kpi: KPITruth
  signal: SignalTruth
  drivers: DriverHypothesis[]
  evidence: EvidencePackage

  persona: Persona
  businessContext?: BusinessContext
}

The exact fields can evolve slightly based on the final M1–M4 contracts.

9. Why IntelligenceContext Is Important

Without it, Module 5 will start independently querying:

database
documents

LLM

and you will recreate logic that already exists.

Instead:

M1
+
M2
+
M3
+
M4
       ↓
IntelligenceContext
       ↓
M5

This is the correct boundary.

11. Story Engine

Module 5's first real engine should be:

src/server/story/

with:

context.ts
story.ts
persona.ts
recommendation.ts
decision.ts
12. Story Engine Responsibilities

The story engine should transform:

facts
+
drivers
+
evidence
+
uncertainty

into:

headline
+
summary
+
what happened
+
why
+
evidence
+
uncertainty
+
alternatives
13. Story Structure

The story should follow a fixed structure:

1. Headline

2. Executive summary

3. What happened?

4. Why did it happen?

5. Primary drivers

6. Supporting evidence

7. Contradictory evidence

8. What remains uncertain?

9. Alternative explanations

10. Recommended action

11. Expected impact

12. Monitoring plan

The target IntelligenceOutput explicitly includes these elements.

14. Headline

Example:

Revenue declined 17.8% in August, primarily driven by lower order volume and elevated stockouts.

This should be generated from verified data.

Not:

"Revenue collapsed because customers hate the new pricing."

unless the evidence actually supports that.

15. Executive Summary

The executive summary should answer:

What changed?
Why?
How confident are we?
What should happen next?

Example:

Revenue fell 17.8% in August. The strongest supported drivers are a 15.2% decline in orders and increased stockout activity. Evidence confidence is high for the order-volume explanation but moderate for stockouts. Immediate focus should be on restoring availability in affected SKUs while monitoring order recovery.

16. What Happened

This section comes directly from:

Module 1
+
Module 2

Example:

Revenue:
₹924.8M → ₹760.5M

MoM:
−17.77%

Signal:
INVESTIGATE

Materiality:
HIGH

No LLM calculation.

17. Why

This section comes primarily from:

Module 3
+
Module 4

Example:

Primary:
Orders decline

Secondary:
Stockouts

Possible:
Discounting

The ranking comes from the upstream analytical/evidence pipeline.

18. Driver Ranking

Suppose M3 returns:

Orders:
91.6%

Stockouts:
86.6%

Discounting:
86.2%

and M4 updates them to:

Orders:
88%

Stockouts:
72%

Discounting:
51%

Module 5 should present the evidence-adjusted ranking.

19. Primary vs Alternative Explanations

Do not present:

Orders caused everything.

Instead:

Primary explanation:
Orders decline

Secondary explanation:
Stockouts

Alternative:
Discounting/product mix

This is especially important when evidence is mixed.

20. Evidence Attachment

Every major narrative claim should map to evidence.

Example:

Claim:
Orders declined substantially.

Evidence:
Sales transaction data

Citation:
sales_transactions
August 2026

Another:

Claim:
Stockout activity increased.

Evidence:
Operations Report — August

Citation:
Operations source
21. Evidence-Grounded Generation

The LLM should receive:

verified context

not direct database access.

Flow:

M1
M2
M3
M4
 ↓
IntelligenceContext
 ↓
Prompt Builder
 ↓
LLM

The implementation plan explicitly states that the LLM should receive the verified context rather than query the database itself.

22. LLM Role

The LLM may handle:

✓ narrative wording
✓ summarisation
✓ persona adaptation
✓ contextual explanation
✓ recommendation phrasing

The LLM must not determine:

❌ KPI values
❌ statistical significance
❌ driver contribution
❌ evidence score
❌ confidence
❌ source freshness
❌ business ownership

Those must come from structured logic.

23. Deterministic vs LLM Processing

The demo should explicitly show:

Task	Method
KPI calculation	SQL / deterministic
Signal detection	Statistics / rules
Driver analysis	M3 analytical engine
Evidence retrieval	M4 retrieval
Evidence score	M4 deterministic
Confidence	M3/M4
Story structure	Deterministic
Narrative wording	LLM
Persona adaptation	LLM + rules
Recommendation catalogue	Deterministic
Recommendation wording	LLM
Priority	Deterministic
Owner	Rule/catalogue
Monitoring	Rule/catalogue
Decision logging	Deterministic

This is exactly the kind of LLM/non-LLM distinction required by Round 2.

24. Persona Engine

Start with only:

Executive
Analyst

The latest plan explicitly recommends implementing these two first rather than immediately building six personas.

25. Critical Persona Principle

Changing persona must not recompute the analysis.

Correct:

                 Same IntelligenceContext
                         │
                ┌────────┴────────┐
                ▼                 ▼
            Executive           Analyst
             Story               Story

Not:

Executive → new analytics
Analyst   → different analytics

The plan explicitly states that only presentation should change.

26. Executive Persona

Executive needs:

short
high-level
decision-oriented
risk-aware
action-oriented

Example:

Revenue declined 17.8% in August.

Primary drivers:
• Orders −15.2%
• Stockouts increased

Recommendation:
Prioritize replenishment of affected SKUs.

Confidence:
88%
27. Analyst Persona

Analyst needs:

more detail
metrics
contributions
evidence
method
contradictions
lineage

Example:

Revenue:
−17.77%

Orders:
−15.24%
Contribution:
81.6%

Evidence confidence:
88%

Evidence:
• sales_transactions
• August operations report

Contradictory evidence:
South-region stockouts remained stable.
28. Regional/Functional Persona

This can be Tier 2.

Examples:

Regional Manager
Marketing Manager
Operations Manager
Commercial Manager

They should see the same analytical truth but emphasise relevant drivers/actions.

29. Persona Configuration

Create:

interface PersonaConfig {
  id: string

  name: string

  verbosity: "low" | "medium" | "high"

  preferredSections: string[]

  allowedActions?: string[]

  defaultDelivery?: string
}
30. Recommendation Engine

This is one of the most important parts of Module 5.

The target chain is:

Driver
   ↓
Controllable lever
   ↓
Action
   ↓
Expected impact
   ↓
Owner
   ↓
Confidence
   ↓
Monitoring

This directly matches the BusinessIntelligence.ai requirement.

31. Recommendation Is NOT Free-Form LLM Generation

Do not allow:

LLM:
"Maybe launch a massive new campaign."

Instead:

Driver
 ↓
Recommendation Catalogue
 ↓
Valid Lever
 ↓
Valid Action
 ↓
LLM phrasing

The implementation plan explicitly recommends a recommendation catalogue.

32. Recommendation Catalogue

Example:

Driver	Controllable Lever
Stockouts	Inventory replenishment
Price	Pricing
Conversion	Funnel optimisation
Marketing	Campaign allocation
Refunds	Returns process
Delivery	Logistics
Product mix	Assortment optimisation
AOV	Cross-sell / bundle strategy
33. Recommendation Structure

Use:

interface Recommendation {
  driver: string

  lever: string

  action: string

  priority: "high" | "medium" | "low"

  confidence: number

  expectedImpact: string

  owner: string

  monitoring: MonitoringPlan[]
}
34. Example Recommendation

Input:

Driver:
Stockouts

Confidence:
88%

Evidence:
Inventory
+
Operations
+
Support

Output:

{
  "driver": "Stockouts",
  "lever": "Inventory replenishment",
  "action": "Prioritize replenishment for affected SKUs",
  "owner": "Supply Chain",
  "priority": "high",
  "confidence": 0.88,
  "monitoring": [
    "Stockout rate",
    "Order fulfillment",
    "Revenue"
  ]
}

This example is already defined in the implementation plan.

35. Recommendation Confidence

Recommendation confidence should depend on:

driver confidence
+
evidence confidence
+
controllability

Conceptually:

Recommendation confidence
=
f(
  driver confidence,
  evidence confidence,
  controllability
)
36. High-Confidence Recommendation

Example:

Driver confidence:
0.90

Evidence confidence:
0.85

Controllable:
YES

→ strong recommendation.

37. Low-Confidence Recommendation

Example:

Evidence confidence:
0.32

Do not recommend:

Immediately change pricing.

Instead:

Investigate further.

This is explicitly required by the current plan.

38. Expected Impact

This is a dangerous area.

Do not allow the LLM to fabricate numerical impact.

Bad:

This action will increase revenue by ₹1.2M.

unless M1–M3 actually produced analytical support for that number.

39. Safe Expected Impact

Use qualitative statements where numerical estimates are unavailable:

Potentially reduce stockout-related lost sales.

or:

Expected impact:
To be estimated after SKU-level simulation.

This rule is explicitly defined in the plan.

40. Impact Types

Use:

high
medium
low
unknown

If there is no analytical basis:

expectedImpactType = "unknown"

rather than inventing a number.

41. Recommendation Priority

Priority should be deterministic.

Example:

HIGH:
high impact + confidence >= 0.80

MEDIUM:
confidence >= 0.60

LOW:
low confidence / low impact

The implementation plan gives this exact conceptual rule.

42. Owner Mapping

Create a deterministic mapping:

Stockouts
→ Supply Chain

Pricing
→ Commercial

Conversion
→ Growth / Marketing

Marketing
→ Marketing

Refunds
→ Customer Operations

Delivery
→ Logistics

The LLM should not invent an arbitrary owner.

43. Monitoring Plan

Every recommendation should have:

metric
target
frequency
owner
stop condition

Example:

{
  "metric": "Stockout rate",
  "target": "< 10%",
  "frequency": "daily",
  "owner": "Supply Chain",
  "stopCondition": "Stockout rate does not improve after 7 days"
}

This is explicitly defined in the current plan.

44. Recommendation Lifecycle

The complete lifecycle becomes:

Driver
 ↓
Evidence
 ↓
Business implication
 ↓
Controllable lever
 ↓
Action
 ↓
Priority
 ↓
Owner
 ↓
Monitoring
 ↓
Human decision
 ↓
Outcome
45. Decision Workspace

This is where TRACE moves beyond "AI-generated insight."

The user should be able to:

Accept
Reject
Modify
Challenge
Override

The current plan explicitly defines these decision interactions.

46. Recommended UI
┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ RECOMMENDED ACTION                         │
│                                            │
│ Prioritize replenishment for affected      │
│ SKUs in North.                             │
│                                            │
│ Owner: Supply Chain                        │
│ Priority: HIGH                             │
│ Confidence: 88%                            │
│                                            │
│ Expected impact:                           │
│ Reduce stockout-related lost sales         │
│                                            │
│ Monitor:                                   │
│ • Stockout rate                            │
│ • Order fulfillment                        │
│ • Revenue                                  │
│                                            │
│ [ Accept ] [ Reject ] [ Modify ]           │
│                                            │
│ [ Challenge Conclusion ]                   │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The implementation plan already describes essentially this UI.

47. Challenge Conclusion

Add:

[ Challenge Conclusion ]

The user can challenge:

□ Driver
□ Evidence
□ Data
□ Confidence
□ Recommendation

and add:

Comment
Correct driver
Additional context

This workflow is already defined in the current plan.

48. Challenge Workflow

The important architecture is:

USER CHALLENGES
      ↓
What is disputed?
      ↓
New EvidenceRequest
      ↓
MODULE 4
      ↓
New EvidencePackage
      ↓
MODULE 5
      ↓
Updated Story
      ↓
Updated Recommendation

This is much stronger than simply allowing the user to edit text.

49. Example Challenge

TRACE says:

Stockouts are a major contributor.

Analyst clicks:

Challenge → Driver

and says:

"Stockouts were normal in North. I believe the issue was pricing."

TRACE should create:

New hypothesis:
Pricing

Evidence request:
pricing data
discount data
competitor pricing

Then send it back to M4.

50. Accept

If user clicks:

Accept

store:

decisionState:
accepted

along with:

analysisId
recommendationId
user
timestamp
51. Reject

If:

Reject

capture:

reason
comment

Do not simply delete the recommendation.

52. Modify

If user modifies:

Prioritize replenishment

to:

Prioritize replenishment only for high-value SKUs

store:

original recommendation
+
modified recommendation
+
user
+
timestamp
53. Override

Override is different from modification.

Example:

TRACE:
High priority

Manager:
Low priority due to budget constraints

Store:

AI priority:
HIGH

Human priority:
LOW

Override:
TRUE

This becomes valuable governance information for Module 6.

54. Decision State

Use:

pending
accepted
rejected
modified
challenged
overridden
55. Decision Logging

Store at least:

analysisId
recommendationId
userId
action
reason
comment
timestamp
56. Decision Memory

Module 5 should remember previous decisions.

Example:

Current:
Revenue decline

Previous:
Same region
Similar decline
3 months ago

Previous action:
Promotional campaign

Outcome:
Revenue recovered 8%

TRACE can surface:

A similar decline previously responded positively to a promotional campaign.

But it must not say:

Promotional campaign will definitely recover revenue.

Historical decisions are context, not truth.

57. Decision Memory Table

Create:

decisions

Potential fields:

id
analysis_id

kpi
period
region

primary_driver

recommendation
owner

decision_state
decision_reason

expected_impact
actual_outcome

feedback

created_at
updated_at
58. Decision Outcome

The important distinction:

Recommendation
      ↓
Decision
      ↓
Action taken
      ↓
Outcome

This enables future learning.

59. Historical Retrieval

For the prototype, do not create another vector database.

Use deterministic retrieval:

SELECT *
FROM decisions
WHERE kpi = 'revenue'
AND primary_driver = 'stockouts'
ORDER BY created_at DESC
LIMIT 5;

The implementation plan explicitly recommends this approach initially.

60. Decision Memory RAG

Embeddings for decisions can be added later.

For MVP:

metric
+
region
+
driver
+
time

is enough.

61. Narrative Versioning

Generated narratives must be versioned.

Store:

model
promptVersion
generatedAt
story

The plan explicitly requires this because a future prompt change should not make the historical output irreproducible.

62. Narrative Cache

Optional:

contextHash
+
persona
+
model
+
promptVersion

→ cached narrative.

This should only be implemented after the main flow works.

The current plan classifies this as optional.

63. LLM Provider Abstraction

Create:

src/server/llm/provider.ts

Interface:

interface LLMProvider {
  generate<T>(
    prompt: string,
    schema: unknown
  ): Promise<T>
}

This avoids tying Module 5 to one model provider.

64. LLM Configuration

Use environment variables.

Example:

OPENAI_API_KEY
LLM_MODEL
LLM_TEMPERATURE
LLM_MAX_TOKENS

Do not hard-code API keys.

65. Structured LLM Output

Do not accept arbitrary text as the authoritative Module 5 object.

Instead:

LLM
 ↓
JSON
 ↓
Schema validation
 ↓
Grounding checks
 ↓
IntelligenceOutput

The current plan explicitly requires structured output validation.

66. LLM Hallucination Protection

The LLM should only be allowed to use:

IntelligenceContext

as factual context.

If the context says:

Revenue decline = 17.77%

the LLM cannot output:

Revenue declined 22%.

Validate important numerical fields.

67. Numerical Grounding

Important numeric claims should ideally be generated from deterministic templates rather than generated freely.

For example:

Revenue declined {{changePct}}%.

where:

changePct

comes directly from M1.

68. Narrative Template Strategy

Use a hybrid:

Deterministic facts
+
LLM language

Example:

[DETERMINISTIC]
Revenue ↓17.77%

[LLM]
"The decline appears primarily associated with..."

This is much safer than fully free-form generation.

69. Uncertainty Communication

If M4 returns:

status:
insufficient_evidence

Module 5 must preserve that.

It should say:

Evidence is insufficient to confidently attribute the decline to competition.

Not:

Competition caused the decline.

70. Mixed Evidence

If M4 says:

status:
mixed

Module 5 should communicate:

Evidence is mixed. While operations data indicates increased stockouts, another source reports stable availability. The stockout hypothesis therefore remains plausible but not conclusive.

71. Sparse History

If M3/M4 indicate:

sparse history

Module 5 should say:

Confidence is limited because only three months of historical observations are available.

This directly supports the Round 2 sparse-history requirement.

72. Insufficient Evidence

If:

confidence < threshold

then recommendation should become:

Investigate further

rather than a high-impact operational action.

73. Alternative Explanations

Example:

Primary:
Orders decline

Alternative:
Stockouts

Secondary:
Pricing

The user should see why alternatives were not selected as primary:

lower evidence confidence
contradictory evidence
lower contribution
74. Citation Rendering

Narrative claims should be linked to:

EvidencePackage

Example:

Orders declined 15.24%. [Sales transaction data]

Clicking the citation should open:

source
document
chunk
date
method
score
75. Evidence Graph Integration

Module 5 should not rebuild the evidence graph.

It consumes M4's:

EvidencePackage
+
Evidence Graph

and surfaces it in the story.

76. Story → Evidence Mapping

A strong implementation should internally maintain:

storyClaimId
    ↓
hypothesisId
    ↓
evidenceIds

Example:

claim_01
 ↓
H1 Orders
 ↓
E12
E17
E24

This creates traceability from narrative to evidence.

77. Recommendation → Evidence Mapping

Similarly:

recommendationId
 ↓
driver
 ↓
evidence
 ↓
confidence

So the system can answer:

Why did TRACE recommend this action?

78. Recommendation → Driver Mapping

Example:

Stockouts
 ↓
Inventory replenishment
 ↓
Supply Chain

This is much more defensible than:

LLM generated:
"Run a promotion."
79. Recommendation Constraints

Every recommendation should validate:

driver exists
driver is sufficiently supported
lever exists
action exists
owner exists
confidence valid
monitoring exists

If any critical field is missing:

do not generate recommendation

or return:

investigate further
80. Business Decision Rights

Eventually recommendations should respect:

persona
role
region
function
decision authority

Example:

Analyst:
Can recommend

Regional Manager:
Can approve regional action

Executive:
Can approve strategic action

For the hackathon, basic role-aware recommendation display is sufficient.

81. Security

Module 5 inherits M6 governance/security, but must still respect:

user
role
region
domain
data access

A North-region manager must not receive a recommendation based on restricted South-region evidence.

82. Cost Control

LLM should not be called repeatedly for:

same context
same persona
same model
same prompt version

Use:

contextHash

for caching when possible.

83. LLM Call Minimisation

Preferred:

1 LLM call
→ complete structured narrative

rather than:

LLM call for headline
+
LLM call for summary
+
LLM call for driver
+
LLM call for recommendation
+
LLM call for monitoring

This reduces:

latency
cost
failure surface
84. LLM Telemetry

Every LLM execution should capture:

model
promptVersion
latency
inputTokens
outputTokens
totalTokens
estimatedCost
success/failure
fallbackUsed

Module 6 will later consume this telemetry.

85. Graceful LLM Failure

If:

LLM unavailable

the pipeline becomes:

IntelligenceContext
       ↓
Deterministic Story
       ↓
Deterministic Recommendation
       ↓
UI

and Module 6 records:

LLM_FAILURE
FALLBACK_USED

This graceful-failure architecture is explicitly defined.

86. Deterministic Story Fallback

Example:

Revenue declined 17.77% in August.

The strongest supported driver is Orders,
which declined 15.24%.

Evidence confidence:
88%.

Recommendation:
Review order-volume recovery and affected segments.

Confidence:
High.

This does not require an LLM.

87. Final IntelligenceOutput

The target contract should be:

interface IntelligenceOutput {
  analysisId: string

  headline: string

  executiveSummary: string

  whatHappened: string

  why: string

  drivers: DriverStory[]

  evidence: EvidenceReference[]

  contradictions: EvidenceReference[]

  uncertainty: UncertaintySection

  alternatives: AlternativeExplanation[]

  recommendation?: Recommendation

  priority?: "high" | "medium" | "low"

  owner?: string

  expectedImpact?: string

  monitoringPlan?: MonitoringPlan[]

  citations: Citation[]

  confidence: number

  decisionState: DecisionState
}

The exact shape should be finalised once M4 is frozen.

88. DriverStory

Example:

interface DriverStory {
  hypothesisId: string
  driver: string

  contribution?: number

  confidence: number

  explanation: string

  supportingEvidenceIds: string[]

  contradictoryEvidenceIds: string[]

  status:
    | "primary"
    | "secondary"
    | "alternative"
}
89. Uncertainty Structure
interface UncertaintySection {
  confidence: number

  status:
    | "high"
    | "medium"
    | "low"
    | "insufficient"

  reasons: string[]

  evidenceGaps: string[]
}
90. Alternative Explanation
interface AlternativeExplanation {
  driver: string
  confidence: number
  reasonNotPrimary: string
  evidenceIds: string[]
}
91. Monitoring Plan
interface MonitoringPlan {
  metric: string
  target: string
  frequency: string
  owner: string
  stopCondition: string
}
92. Module 5 API

Recommended:

POST /api/intelligence

Input:

{
  "analysisId": "...",
  "persona": "executive"
}

The server retrieves/receives the M1–M4 context and returns:

IntelligenceOutput
93. Persona API

Could also support:

GET /api/intelligence/:analysisId?persona=executive

Important:

persona change
≠
new analytical computation

Only presentation changes.

94. Recommendation API
POST /api/recommendations

Input:

analysisId

Output:

Recommendation[]

But ideally recommendation generation is part of the main M5 pipeline rather than an isolated service.

95. Decision API
POST /api/decision

Example:

{
  "analysisId": "...",
  "recommendationId": "...",
  "action": "accept",
  "comment": "Proceed with high-value SKUs first."
}
96. Challenge API
POST /api/intelligence/challenge

Input:

{
  "analysisId": "...",
  "target": "driver",
  "comment": "I believe pricing is the main driver.",
  "correctDriver": "pricing"
}

Then:

Challenge
 ↓
M4 new EvidenceRequest
 ↓
EvidencePackage
 ↓
M5 revised output
97. Decision Memory API
GET /api/decisions/similar

Initially deterministic:

metric
region
driver
period

Later:

semantic decision retrieval
98. Database Tables

Recommended Module 5 tables:

decisions
recommendations
story_versions
feedback

Optional:

decision_outcomes
recommendation_catalog
llm_runs
99. Recommendation Catalogue Table

Could use:

recommendation_catalog

with:

id
driver
lever
action_template
owner
default_priority
monitoring_metric
monitoring_target
stop_condition

This makes recommendations configurable instead of hard-coded.

100. Feedback Table

Fields:

Possible:

Fields:

Fields:

id

103. Decision Outcome

Later:

Fields:

decision_id
metric

target

This allows TRACE to eventually learn:

Recommendation
→ Decision
→ Outcome
104. Learning Loop

The long-term architecture:

Insight
 ↓
Recommendation
 ↓
Human decision
 ↓
Action
 ↓
Outcome
 ↓
Feedback
 ↓
Evaluation
 ↓
Future recommendations

This directly addresses the BusinessIntelligence.ai requirement for a mechanism to learn from analyst/business-user feedback.

105. But Do NOT Automatically Retrain

For the hackathon:

feedback
→ stored
→ aggregated
→ evaluated

Do not build:

automatic model retraining

That belongs far beyond the MVP.

106. Recommendation Evaluation

Track:

recommendation acceptance rate
recommendation rejection rate
override rate
challenge rate
outcome success rate

These can later feed Module 6.

107. Story Evaluation

Create evaluation cases:

case
expected headline
expected primary driver
expected evidence
expected uncertainty
expected recommendation

Measure:

grounding
correctness
persona compliance
recommendation validity
108. LLM Evaluation

Do not only ask:

Does the story sound good?

Test:

Does every numerical claim match M1?
Does every evidence claim exist in M4?
Does the recommendation use a valid lever?
Does the owner match the catalogue?
Does uncertainty match M4?
109. Grounding Test

Given:

Revenue:
−17.77%

LLM output:

Revenue:
−20%

→ fail.

110. Recommendation Grounding Test

Given:

driver = stockouts

LLM suggests:

launch a new TV campaign

if not supported by the recommendation catalogue.

→ reject/replace.

111. Low-Evidence Test

Input:

Competition hypothesis
Evidence:
none

Expected:

No confident recommendation.

Suggested next step:
Collect competitor pricing and promotion data.
112. Contradiction Test

M4:

support = 0.80
contradiction = 0.78

M5 should say:

Evidence is mixed.

Not:

Stockouts definitively caused the decline.

113. Persona Test

Same context:

Executive

should produce:

3–5 concise sections

while:

Analyst

shows:

contribution
confidence
evidence
method
contradictions

without changing the underlying truth.

114. Decision Test

Click:

Accept

Expected:

decisionState = accepted

and an audit record.

115. Challenge Test

Click:

Challenge
→ Driver

Expected:

new evidence request

rather than merely editing the displayed story.

116. Override Test

TRACE:

Priority = HIGH

User:

Priority = LOW

Expected:

AI recommendation retained
+
human override recorded
117. Decision Memory Test

Previous:

Stockouts
→ replenishment
→ successful outcome

Current:

Stockouts

Expected:

Similar prior decision available

but explicitly labelled as historical context.

118. LLM Failure Test

Disable API key/provider.

Expected:

Deterministic narrative
+
Deterministic recommendation

and:

fallbackUsed = true
119. Cost Test

Run the same analysis twice.

Expected:

first:
LLM call

second:
cache hit

if narrative caching is enabled.

 ↓

This connects directly to M4.

128. Recommendation Detail

When clicking recommendation:

Driver
 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

This gives TRACE a complete audit chain.

139. The Correct LLM Architecture

Do not do:

User
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
"Why did revenue fall?"
 ↓
LLM queries database
 ↓
LLM retrieves documents
 ↓
LLM recommends action

That would collapse M1–M5 into an opaque agent.

Instead:

M1 ──┐
M2 ──┤
M3 ──┤
M4 ──┘
     ↓
IntelligenceContext
     ↓
Deterministic story structure
     ↓
Recommendation catalogue
     ↓
LLM
     ↓
Validated IntelligenceOutput
140. The Most Important Recommendation Rule

The LLM should phrase recommendations, not invent business levers.

Correct:

Stockouts
 ↓
Inventory replenishment
 ↓
Valid action
 ↓
LLM:
"Prioritize replenishment for affected SKUs."

Incorrect:

Stockouts
 ↓
LLM:
"Launch a 30% discount campaign."

unless the recommendation catalogue/analytics explicitly permits it.

141. The Most Important Narrative Rule

The LLM should explain verified facts, not create facts.

Correct:

M1:
Revenue −17.77%

M3:
Orders major driver

M4:
Evidence supports Orders

M5:
"Revenue declined primarily alongside a substantial decline in orders."

Incorrect:

LLM:
"Customers switched to competitors because prices increased."

when no evidence supports it.

142. The Most Important Uncertainty Rule

If M4 says:

insufficient_evidence

M5 must not turn it into:

high-confidence narrative

The uncertainty must survive all the way to the UI.
