152. Final Conceptual Definition

I would lock this sentence into Implementation_plan.txt:

Module 5 is the Intelligence and Decision layer of TRACE. It consumes verified analytical and evidence outputs from Modules 1–4, converts them into persona-specific, evidence-grounded intelligence, generates constrained and confidence-aware recommendations, and provides a decision workspace that captures human decisions, challenges, feedback, and historical outcomes.

The latest implementation plan already converges on essentially this definition.

Bottom line

Module 5 should be the layer where TRACE finally becomes an actual BusinessIntelligence.ai product rather than an analytics pipeline.

The architecture should remain:

M1
KPI truth
 ↓
M2
Signal
 ↓
M3
Drivers
 ↓
M4
Evidence
 ↓
M5
Story + Recommendation + Decision
 ↓
M6
Governance

And the single most important implementation rule is:

M4 EvidencePackage
        ↓
IntelligenceContext
        ↓
Deterministic Story
        ↓
Deterministic Recommendation
        ↓
UI
        ↓
LLM enhancement

Do not start with the LLM. The deterministic path must work first, with the LLM acting as an enhancement and fallback existing in the opposite direction. That is the architecture explicitly recommended in the current TRACE implementation plan.

The result should be that a judge can click through one complete chain:

"Revenue fell 17.77% → here is why → here is the evidence → here is what remains uncertain → here is the recommended action → here is who owns it → here is how we will monitor it → here is what the human decided."

That is the strongest M5 contribution to the Round 2 requirement of moving TRACE from "AI explains the dashboard" to "AI investigates and helps the business make a defensible decision."




















TRACE — MODULE 6 IMPLEMENTATION PLAN
Governance, Security, Audit, Evaluation, Feedback & Observability

Status: 🟡 Architecture substantially defined
Implementation: 🔴 Largely pending
Module: Module 6

Primary question

"Can we prove and evaluate everything TRACE did?"

More precisely:

Can we prove what TRACE did, why it did it, what data and models were involved, who accessed the information, how reliable the result was, what it cost, and what happened after the decision?

This is the definition already established in the current implementation plan.

1. Where Module 6 Fits

The final TRACE chain should be:

                         TRACE
                           │
                           ▼
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M1      │
                    │ Governed    │
                    │ Data + KPI  │
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M2      │
                    │ Signal /    │
                    │ Materiality│
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M3      │
                    │ Drivers /   │
                    │ Hypotheses  │
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M4      │
                    │ Evidence /  │
                    │ RAG         │
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M5      │
                    │ Intelligence│
                    │ + Decision  │
                    └──────┬──────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                         HUMAN
                           │
                    Decision / Feedback
                           │
                           ▼
              ┌────────────────────────┐
              │         M6             │
              │ Governance             │
              │ Security               │
              │ Audit                  │
              │ Evaluation             │
              │ Feedback               │
              │ Cost / Quality         │
              │ Drift                  │
              └────────────────────────┘
                           ▲
                           │
                           │ telemetry
                           │
                    ┌─────────────┐
                    │     M7      │
                    │ Runtime/Ops │
                    └─────────────┘

The important architectural correction is:

M6 is not merely "the module after M5". It is a cross-cutting governance layer observing M1–M5, while M7 provides operational runtime telemetry.

2. Core Principle
Every important TRACE insight must be reconstructable

Suppose a Regional Manager asks:

Why did North revenue fall?

TRACE should eventually be able to reconstruct:

USER
Regional Manager
        │
        ▼
QUESTION
Why did North revenue fall?
        │
        ▼
M1
Revenue = ₹9.12M
Definition = governed
Source = ERP
        │
        ▼
M2
Revenue ↓ 9.9%
Materiality = HIGH
Method = statistical threshold
        │
        ▼
M3
AOV decline = primary driver
Confidence = 79%
        │
        ▼
M4
3 supporting sources
1 contradiction
Evidence confidence = 86%
        │
        ▼
M5
Recommendation generated
        │
        ▼
HUMAN
Recommendation accepted
        │
        ▼
M6
Complete audit record

That end-to-end reconstruction is the central purpose of M6.

3. What Module 6 Owns

Freeze this boundary.

Security
Authentication
Authorization
Role-based access control
Data entitlements
Row-level access
Column-level access
Domain-level access
Security event logging
Auditability
Audit logging
Analysis-run tracking
Module-run tracking
Data lineage
Data provenance
Decision history
AI Governance
Model version
Prompt version
LLM usage
Token usage
LLM cost
LLM latency
Fallback usage
Evaluation
Evaluation datasets
Module evaluation
Retrieval evaluation
Driver evaluation
Narrative evaluation
Recommendation evaluation
Confidence calibration
Monitoring
Data drift
Evidence/retrieval drift
Model drift
Narrative quality
Recommendation quality
System health
Runtime performance
Governance UI
Governance dashboard
Audit explorer
Evaluation dashboard
Drift dashboard
System-health view

These responsibilities are explicitly defined in the current M6 specification.

4. What Module 6 Must NOT Own

This is extremely important.

M6 must not become another analytics engine.

❌ KPI calculation
❌ KPI definitions
❌ anomaly detection
❌ statistical testing
❌ driver decomposition
❌ contribution calculation
❌ hypothesis generation
❌ RAG retrieval
❌ vector search
❌ evidence scoring
❌ evidence classification
❌ narrative generation
❌ recommendation generation

Instead:

M1 → computes
M2 → detects
M3 → explains analytically
M4 → retrieves/evaluates evidence
M5 → narrates/recommends
M6 → records/evaluates/governs
M7 → operates infrastructure

This separation is explicitly required by the latest plan.

5. The Most Important M6 Architecture

Think of M6 as a governance plane:

                 ┌───────────────────────────┐
                 │          MODULE 6         │
                 │                           │
                 │ Security                  │
                 │ Audit                     │
                 │ Lineage                   │
                 │ Evaluation                │
                 │ Feedback                  │
                 │ Cost                      │
                 │ Quality                   │
                 │ Drift                     │
                 │ Governance UI             │
                 └─────────────┬─────────────┘
                               │
         ┌─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
         │                     │                      │
         ▼                     ▼                      ▼
        M1                    M2                     M3
         │                     │                      │
         └─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                              M4
                               │
                               ▼
                              M5
                               │
                               ▼
                            HUMAN
                               │
                               ▼
                           DECISION

                    M7 ── runtime telemetry ──► M6

This is a much better mental model than:

M5 → M6

because M6 needs information from every module.

6. Module 6 Data Flow

Every important operation should emit an event:

Module executes
      ↓
Module produces result
      ↓
Telemetry/event generated
      ↓
M6 records metadata
      ↓
Audit / evaluation / monitoring

For example:

M3
 ↓
DriverHypothesis
 ↓
confidence = 0.79
 ↓
M6
 ↓
module_runs
7. analysisId — The Backbone

Every investigation should receive a unique:

analysisId

Example:

ANL-2026-0001

Then every module attaches to it:

ANL-2026-0001

├── M1
├── M2
├── M3
├── M4
├── M5
├── HUMAN
└── M6

The current plan explicitly recommends analysisId as the cross-module correlation key.

8. Complete Analysis Trace

Example:

ANL-001
│
├── USER
│   ├── user = regional_manager_01
│   ├── role = regional_manager
│   └── region = North
│
├── M1
│   ├── KPI = Revenue
│   ├── value = 9.12M
│   ├── definition = revenue_v2
│   ├── source = ERP
│   └── freshness = 12 min
│
├── M2
│   ├── change = -9.9%
│   ├── materiality = HIGH
│   ├── method = z_score
│   └── latency = 41ms
│
├── M3
│   ├── driver = AOV
│   ├── contribution = 71%
│   └── confidence = 0.79
│
├── M4
│   ├── evidence_count = 5
│   ├── contradiction_count = 1
│   └── confidence = 0.86
│
├── M5
│   ├── story_version = v3
│   ├── recommendation = ...
│   └── llm_cost = ...
│
└── HUMAN
    ├── action = ACCEPT
    └── timestamp = ...

That is the TRACE audit trail.

9. Authentication

For the hackathon, don't build enterprise SSO.

Use:

synthetic users

Example:

user_001
user_002
user_003

with:

name
role
region
permissions

The current implementation plan explicitly specifies synthetic users for the prototype.

10. Users

Create:

users

Schema:

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  role TEXT NOT NULL,
  region TEXT,
  permissions TEXT,
  created_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

This is already the proposed M6 schema.

11. Prototype Personas

Use a small set:

Executive
Analyst
Regional Manager

You don't need ten enterprise roles.

12. Authorization

Authorization should be checked before data reaches the user.

Example:

Regional Manager
Region = North

Request:

Revenue / North

→ allowed.

Request:

Revenue / South

→ denied or filtered.

13. Row-Level Security

Example:

User:
region = North

Query:

SELECT *
FROM sales_transactions
WHERE region = :user_region;

The important point is that the filter must happen server-side, not merely in the React UI.

14. Column-Level Security

Some information can be hidden:

Analyst:
Revenue
Orders
AOV

while:

Executive:
Revenue
Orders

and sensitive fields such as:

customer_email
customer_phone
internal_margin

remain restricted.

For the prototype, this can be simulated through permission metadata.

15. Domain-Level Security

Example:

Operations Manager
→ operations data

Marketing Manager
→ marketing data

Finance Manager
→ financial data

M6 ensures the user's entitlement is respected.

16. Security Event Logging

Every important security event should be recorded.

Examples:

LOGIN_SUCCESS
LOGIN_FAILURE
ACCESS_GRANTED
ACCESS_DENIED
DATA_EXPORT
UNAUTHORIZED_REGION_ACCESS
17. Audit Logging

Create:

audit_logs

Conceptual fields:

id
analysis_id
user_id
event_type
module
action
resource_type
resource_id
metadata
timestamp

Example:

{
  "analysisId": "ANL-001",
  "userId": "user_001",
  "eventType": "RECOMMENDATION_ACCEPTED",
  "module": "M5",
  "timestamp": "..."
}
18. Audit Event Taxonomy

Define fixed event types rather than arbitrary strings.

AUTH
 ├── LOGIN
 ├── LOGOUT
 └── LOGIN_FAILURE

ACCESS
 ├── DATA_ACCESS
 ├── ACCESS_DENIED
 └── EXPORT

ANALYSIS
 ├── ANALYSIS_STARTED
 ├── ANALYSIS_COMPLETED
 └── ANALYSIS_FAILED

MODULE
 ├── MODULE_STARTED
 ├── MODULE_COMPLETED
 └── MODULE_FAILED

AI
 ├── LLM_CALL
 ├── LLM_FAILURE
 └── FALLBACK_USED

DECISION
 ├── ACCEPT
 ├── REJECT
 ├── MODIFY
 ├── OVERRIDE
 └── CHALLENGE

FEEDBACK
 ├── DRIVER_CORRECTION
 ├── EVIDENCE_CORRECTION
 └── NARRATIVE_FEEDBACK
19. Module Run Tracking

Create:

module_runs

Each execution records:

analysis_id
module
start_time
end_time
latency_ms
status
error
input_version
output_version

Example:

{
  "analysisId": "ANL-001",
  "module": "M3",
  "latencyMs": 124,
  "status": "success"
}
20. Why Module Run Tracking Matters

A judge can click:

M3

and see:

Started:
14:32:10

Completed:
14:32:10.124

Latency:
124ms

Status:
SUCCESS

This is far more convincing than a static dashboard.

21. Provenance

Where did this value originate?

Example:

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓
M1
 ↓
M2
 ↓
M3
 ↓
M4
 ↓

22. Data Lineage

 ↓

23. Immutable-ish Audit Design

For the prototype, audit events should be append-only.

Do not allow:

old decision
→ UPDATE
→ hide history

Instead:

Decision:
ACCEPTED

Later:
OVERRIDDEN

Both remain in history.
24. Decision Audit

M5 sends:

recommendation
decision
feedback

M6 records:

who
what
when
why
original recommendation
final action
25. Example Decision Audit
Recommendation:
Prioritize replenishment

AI confidence:
88%

Human:
Override

Human decision:
Focus only on Tier-1 SKUs

Reason:
Budget constraint

Timestamp:
2026-08-27 14:32

This is a high-value governance feature.

26. LLM Governance

M6 does not call the LLM.

It monitors LLM usage.

The architecture is:

M5
 ↓
LLM Gateway / Provider
 ↓
LLM response
 ↓
Telemetry
 ↓
M6

Likewise M4 may use LLM assistance, and M6 monitors it.

27. LLM Call Tracking

Create:

llm_calls

Schema already proposed:

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS llm_calls (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
  analysis_id TEXT,
  module TEXT,
  model TEXT,
  prompt_version TEXT,
  input_tokens INTEGER,
  output_tokens INTEGER,
  latency_ms INTEGER,
  estimated_cost REAL,
  success INTEGER,
  created_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

28. What to Track Per LLM Call
analysisId
module
provider
model
promptVersion
inputTokens
outputTokens
totalTokens
latency
estimatedCost
success
fallback
timestamp
29. API Key Security

Never log:

OPENAI_API_KEY

or any provider credential.

Correct:

Frontend
   ↓
Next.js API
   ↓
M5
   ↓
LLM Provider

not:

Frontend
   ↓
OPENAI_API_KEY

The current implementation plan explicitly specifies server-side key handling and prohibits exposing keys through M6 audit logs.

30. Prompt Versioning

Store:

prompt_version

Example:

story_prompt_v1
story_prompt_v2

Suppose a narrative changes after you modify the prompt.

M6 should be able to answer:

Which prompt generated the old narrative?

31. Model Versioning

Store:

model

Example:

model = gpt-5.x

along with the provider and execution timestamp.

32. Why Model + Prompt Versioning Matters

Suppose:

August 20:
model A
prompt v1

and:

August 27:
model B
prompt v2

produces different narratives.

M6 can distinguish:

data changed

from:

model/prompt changed
33. Evaluation Framework

This is one of the strongest components of M6.

Don't simply say:

"Our AI is accurate."

Build an actual evaluation dataset.

34. Evaluation Dataset

Create:

public/data/evaluation.json

Start with:

10 scenarios

Then expand to:

20–30 scenarios

if time allows.

35. Evaluation Case Structure

Example:

{
  "id": "eval_001",
  "metric": "revenue",
  "period": "2026-08",
  "expectedSignal": "INVESTIGATE",
  "expectedPrimaryDriver": "orders",
  "expectedEvidenceStatus": "supported",
  "expectedAbstention": false
}
36. What M6 Evaluates
M2
signal accuracy
materiality classification
M3
driver ranking
contribution accuracy
confidence
M4
retrieval precision
retrieval recall
evidence coverage
support/contradiction
M5
grounding
narrative quality
recommendation validity
persona compliance
37. M2 Evaluation

Example:

Expected:
INVESTIGATE

Actual:
INVESTIGATE

→ PASS

For 10 scenarios:

9 / 10 correct

→

90% signal accuracy
38. M3 Evaluation

Suppose expected driver:

Orders

TRACE returns:

Orders

→ correct.

If TRACE returns:

Marketing

→ error.

Track:

Top-1 accuracy
Top-3 accuracy
39. M4 Evaluation

For retrieval:

Relevant documents:
E1
E4
E7

Retrieved:

E1
E4
E8

Then:

Precision
Recall

can be calculated.

40. M5 Grounding Evaluation

Check:

Does the narrative claim match M1?
Does the driver match M3?
Does the evidence match M4?
Does confidence match upstream outputs?

Example:

M1:
Revenue −17.77%

M5:
Revenue −17.77%

→ PASS.

If:

M5:
Revenue −21%

→ FAIL.

41. Recommendation Evaluation

Check:

driver
lever
action
owner
priority
confidence
monitoring

against the expected output.

42. Evaluation Runs

Create:

evaluation_runs

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS evaluation_runs (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
  dataset_version TEXT,
  module TEXT,
  metric TEXT,
  score REAL,

  created_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

43. Evaluation Versioning

Use:

dataset_v1
dataset_v2

So you know:

Model A
+
evaluation dataset v1
=
92%

versus:

Model B
+
dataset v2
=
95%
44. Confidence Calibration

M6 should check whether:

confidence = 0.90

actually corresponds roughly to:

90% reliability

For the prototype, even a simple calibration plot/table is sufficient.

45. Example Calibration
Predicted confidence | Actual correctness
---------------------|-------------------
0.5–0.6              | 55%
0.6–0.7              | 67%
0.7–0.8              | 75%
0.8–0.9              | 84%
0.9–1.0              | 93%

This makes confidence much more meaningful than simply displaying a percentage.

46. Abstention Monitoring

Track:

total analyses
abstentions
abstention rate

Example:

100 analyses
12 abstentions

Abstention rate:
12%

This is particularly relevant because Round 2 explicitly requires a low-confidence scenario where TRACE clarifies or abstains.

47. Feedback Loop

M5:

Recommendation

Human:

Accept
Reject
Modify
Challenge

M6:

records feedback

Then:

feedback
 ↓
error analysis
 ↓
prompt/rule improvement
 ↓
evaluation

The current plan explicitly defines this loop.

48. Feedback Table

Create:

feedback

Potential fields:

id

CORRECT
INCORRECT

WRONG_CONFIDENCE
WRONG_NARRATIVE
50. Human Feedback Should Not Directly Change Truth

Suppose an analyst says:

"The driver is pricing."

Do not immediately overwrite:

M3:
Orders

Instead:

Analyst correction
       ↓
feedback
       ↓
new hypothesis/evidence request
       ↓
M4
       ↓
re-evaluation

This preserves analytical integrity.

51. Recommendation Acceptance Rate

Track:

accepted
rejected
modified
overridden

Example:

Recommendations:
100

Accepted:
72

Rejected:
15

Modified:
8

Overridden:
5
52. Override Rate

A very interesting governance metric:

override rate
=
overridden recommendations
/
total recommendations

If:

20%

of recommendations are overridden, investigate why.

Potentially:

poor recommendation quality
missing business context
low confidence calibration
53. Challenge Rate

Track:

challenge rate

A high challenge rate may indicate:

weak evidence
wrong driver ranking
poor narrative
54. Recommendation Outcome Monitoring

Eventually:

Recommendation
 ↓
Decision
 ↓
Action
 ↓
Actual KPI outcome

Example:

Recommendation:
Reduce stockouts

Expected:
Improved revenue

Actual:
Revenue +8.2%

Store the outcome.

56. Data Drift

28,000

21,000

Potential drift.

57. Drift Metrics Table

Already defined:

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS drift_metrics (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
  metric_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  metric_type TEXT NOT NULL,
  baseline_value REAL,
  current_value REAL,
  drift_score REAL,
  status TEXT,
  created_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

59. Data Drift Example
Baseline:
Orders mean = 28,817

Current:
Orders mean = 19,400

Drift:
HIGH

M6 could display:

Orders distribution has materially shifted from the baseline period.

60. Evidence Drift

M4 previously retrieved:

5 strong sources

Now:

2 relevant sources
3 weak sources

M6 can flag:

Evidence quality declining
61. Model Drift

If the model changes:

Model A
→ 91% evaluation score

then:

Model B
→ 84%

M6 should flag:

Model quality regression
62. Narrative Quality Monitoring

hallucination rate

64. System Health

M6 should provide a high-level health state:

HEALTHY
DEGRADED
UNHEALTHY

Example:

Database ✓
M2 ✓
M3 ✓
M4 ✓
LLM ✗

System:
DEGRADED

Not:

UNHEALTHY

because deterministic M5 fallback may still work.

The current architecture explicitly uses this degradation model.

65. M6 vs M7 Boundary

This needs to be frozen.

Capability	M6	M7
Authentication	✓	Support
Authorization	✓	Middleware
Audit	✓	Emit events
Evaluation	✓	Runtime data
Drift	✓	Source/runtime data
KPI	✗	✗
Anomaly	✗	✗
Driver analysis	✗	✗
RAG	✗	✗
LLM narrative	✗	✗
Deployment	✗	✓
Retry	✗	✓
Timeout	✗	✓
Cache	✗	✓
Rate limiting	✗	✓
Scheduler	✗	✓
Runtime telemetry	Consume	Produce
Performance	Evaluate	Measure
Fallback	Govern	Execute

This separation is explicitly established in the current plan.

66. M7 → M6 Example

M7 detects:

LLM timeout

M7:

retry
fallback

M6 records:

LLM_TIMEOUT
FALLBACK_USED

Then M6 evaluates:

Fallback frequency:
12%

Impact:
Narrative quality −3%

M7 executes.

M6 governs.

contradictions

M6 evaluates:

recall
retrieval quality
evidence coverage
latency

M6 does not perform the retrieval itself.

84. Failure Monitoring

85. Failure Example
M4:
RETRIEVAL_FAILURE

Fallback:
Structured evidence only

Impact:
Evidence confidence unavailable

M5:
Abstained

M6 should record the entire chain.

86. Graceful Failure

The crucial rule:

M6 must never become a dependency that prevents TRACE from functioning.

If M6's database is temporarily unavailable:

TRACE intelligence
should not necessarily stop

The event can be queued/logged later depending on the prototype architecture.

Similarly:

LLM unavailable
 ↓
M5 deterministic fallback
 ↓
M6 records:
LLM_FAILURE
FALLBACK_USED

The current architecture explicitly requires this graceful degradation.

87. Audit vs Operational Logging

Do not dump every technical log into audit_logs.

Separate:

Operational logs
→ M7

Governance/audit events
→ M6

Example:

"HTTP connection reset"

→ M7.

Whereas:

"User attempted access to restricted South-region data"

→ M6.

101. Export Governance

must be audited.

102. PII Protection

Do not place sensitive values into:

audit_logs
llm_calls
evaluation_logs

For example:

customer_email
customer_phone

should not be copied into telemetry.

Use:

customer_id

or anonymized identifiers.

103. Data Minimisation

M6 should record:

what was accessed

rather than unnecessarily copying:

all underlying business data

into audit logs.

104. Governance Event Example
{
  "eventType": "ANALYSIS_COMPLETED",
  "analysisId": "ANL-001",
  "userId": "USR-004",
  "modules": ["M1", "M2", "M3", "M4", "M5"],
  "status": "SUCCESS",
  "durationMs": 1240
}
105. End-to-End Governance Record

For a single insight, M6 should be able to answer:

Who?
Regional Manager
What?
Revenue investigation
Which data?
ERP
Sales
Operations
Which KPI definition?
revenue_v2
Which analytical method?
z-score
contribution analysis
Which evidence?
5 sources
Which model?
LLM model X
Which prompt?
story_prompt_v3
What cost?
₹0.xx
What decision?
Accepted
Who overrode it?
Manager
What happened afterwards?
Revenue recovered 8.2%

That is the actual value of M6.

107. LLM vs Non-LLM Processing Ledger

This would be an excellent M6 feature.

Display:

TRACE PROCESSING BREAKDOWN

M1 KPI calculation
    DETERMINISTIC

M2 anomaly detection
    STATISTICAL

M3 driver contribution
    ANALYTICAL

M4 retrieval
    HYBRID RETRIEVAL

M4 evidence classification
    RULES + OPTIONAL LLM

M5 narrative
    LLM

M5 recommendation priority
    BUSINESS RULES

M5 recommendation phrasing
    LLM

M6 audit
    DETERMINISTIC

This directly demonstrates the requirement that teams explicitly distinguish LLM from non-LLM processing.

                   EVALUATION
                        │

The current implementation plan explicitly defines this feedback → error analysis → improvement → re-evaluation loop.

112. Don't Automatically Retrain Models

For the hackathon:

→ identify errors
→ improve prompt/rules
→ rerun evaluation

Do not build:

→ automatic model retraining

That would be unnecessary scope.

116. Recommended Implementation Order

This is the most important practical part.

Phase 1 — analysisId

First create:

analysisId

and make every module propagate it.

M1 → M2 → M3 → M4 → M5

Everything else depends on this.

Phase 2 — Module Run Tracking

Implement:

module_runs

Then wrap every module:

startModuleRun()
    ↓
execute module
    ↓
completeModuleRun()

Record:

latency
status
error
Phase 3 — Audit Logging

Implement:

audit_logs

and an audit event helper:

recordAuditEvent(...)

Use it for:

analysis started
analysis completed
module completed
access denied
recommendation accepted
recommendation rejected
challenge
override
Phase 4 — Authentication + Authorization

Implement:

users
roles
region
permissions

Then protect:

/api/*

server-side.

Phase 5 — Provenance

Connect:

M1 lineage
M2 method
M3 analytical method
M4 evidence provenance
M5 story/recommendation

to:

analysisId
Phase 6 — LLM Telemetry

Once M5 LLM calls work:

LLM call
 ↓
tokens
 ↓
latency
 ↓
model
 ↓
prompt version
 ↓
cost
 ↓
llm_calls

The current plan explicitly marks this as a later integration after M5 LLM functionality exists.

Phase 7 — Evaluation Dataset

Create:

public/data/evaluation.json

Start with:

10 scenarios

Then evaluate:

M2
M3
M4
M5
Phase 8 — Governance Dashboard

First version:

System health
Analysis count
Module latency
LLM cost
Evaluation score
Abstention rate
Override rate

Exactly as recommended in the current plan.

Phase 9 — Drift

Only after the dashboard works:

Data drift
Evidence drift
Model drift
Phase 10 — Feedback

Connect:

M5
 ↓
Human feedback
 ↓
M6
 ↓
Evaluation dataset

Then:

feedback
 ↓
error analysis
 ↓
prompt/rule improvement
 ↓
evaluation
Phase 11 — M7 Integration

Finally:

M7
 ↓
runtime telemetry
 ↓
M6
 ↓
governance metrics

The current plan explicitly distinguishes M7's operational role from M6's governance role.

135. The Most Important Architectural Distinction

The complete TRACE architecture should therefore be understood as:

M1
"What is the number?"
        ↓
M2
"Is the movement meaningful?"
        ↓
M3
"What could explain it?"
        ↓
M4
"What evidence supports or contradicts it?"
        ↓
M5
"What is the defensible story and recommended action?"
        ↓
HUMAN
"What decision was made?"
        ↓
M6
"Can we prove, govern and evaluate everything above?"
        ↑
        │
M7
"Did the system run correctly?"

The latest implementation plan explicitly makes this distinction: M6 governs and evaluates; M7 operates the infrastructure.

If time is limited, I would not try to implement all of M6.

Build this exact vertical slice:

                 USER

                   ▼
             Login / Role

                   ▼

                   ▼
            M1 → M2 → M3
                   │
                   ▼
                  M4
                   │
                   ▼
                  M5
                   │
                   ▼
           Recommendation
                   │
                   ▼
              [ACCEPT]
                   │
                   ▼
                  M6
                   │
       ┌───────────┼───────────┐
       ▼           ▼           ▼

       │           │           │
       ▼           ▼           ▼
  Full Trace    LLM Cost    92.4%
       │
       ▼
 GOVERNANCE DASHBOARD

And then demonstrate:

Scenario A — Normal
Revenue ↓
→ Driver identified
→ Evidence found
→ Recommendation
→ Accepted
→ Complete audit trail
Scenario B — Security
North Manager
→ attempts South data
→ ACCESS DENIED
→ security event logged
Scenario C — Low confidence
Insufficient evidence
→ M5 abstains
→ M6 records abstention
Scenario D — LLM failure
LLM unavailable
→ deterministic fallback
→ M6 records failure + fallback
Scenario E — Feedback
Manager:
"Wrong driver"

→ feedback stored
→ evaluation case created
→ future evaluation

That gives you five extremely strong governance stories without building unnecessary enterprise infrastructure.

137. Final M6 Objective

The one sentence I would put into the authoritative Implementation_plan.txt is:

Module 6 is TRACE's cross-cutting trust and governance layer. It authenticates and authorizes users, records complete analytical and decision provenance, evaluates module and AI quality, monitors cost, latency and drift, captures human feedback, and provides the governance interfaces required to reconstruct and assess every important TRACE decision.

And the ultimate test is:

If a judge asks, "Why should I trust this recommendation?", TRACE should be able to answer without asking the LLM to explain itself.

It should instead show:

DATA
 ↓
KPI DEFINITION
 ↓
SIGNAL
 ↓
ANALYTICAL METHOD
 ↓
DRIVER
 ↓
EVIDENCE
 ↓
CONFIDENCE
 ↓
NARRATIVE
 ↓
RECOMMENDATION
 ↓
MODEL / PROMPT
 ↓
COST / LATENCY
 ↓
HUMAN DECISION
 ↓
OUTCOME

That is what turns TRACE from "an AI dashboard" into a governed enterprise intelligence system. The Round 2 brief's requirements around evidence, lineage, confidence, security, LLM/non-LLM transparency, telemetry, and feedback are all covered by this M6 design.


















1. What Module 7 Actually Is

Module 7 answers:

"Can the whole BusinessIntelligence.ai system reliably run?"

It is the operational layer around M1–M6, not another intelligence module.

The current architecture explicitly describes M7 as cross-cutting infrastructure rather than simply "Module 8 after M6."

6. Phase 1 — Freeze Runtime Contracts

Before writing operational code, define the common contracts.

RequestContext
interface RequestContext {
  requestId: string
  userId?: string
  analysisId?: string
  startedAt: number
}
RuntimePolicy
interface RuntimePolicy {
  timeoutMs: number
  retries: number
  cache?: boolean
  rateLimit?: boolean
}
RuntimeResult
interface RuntimeResult<T> {
  data: T
  durationMs: number
  cacheHit: boolean
  retries: number
  fallbackUsed: boolean
}
RuntimeError
interface RuntimeError {
  code: string
  message: string
  requestId: string
  retryable: boolean
}
HealthStatus
type HealthStatus =
  | "healthy"
  | "degraded"
  | "unhealthy"
TelemetryEvent
interface TelemetryEvent {
  requestId: string
  module?: string
  event: string
  durationMs?: number
  status?: string
  metadata?: Record<string, unknown>
}

The latest plan specifically recommends freezing these contracts before implementation.

7. Phase 2 — Request IDs

Every request gets:

REQ-001
REQ-002
REQ-003

Example:

{
  "requestId": "REQ-001",
  "question": "Why did revenue decline?"
}

Every downstream operation carries it:

REQ-001
 │
 ├── M1
 ├── M2
 ├── M3
 ├── M4
 ├── M5
 └── M6

This gives you correlation across the entire system.

8. analysisId vs requestId

Do not confuse them.

requestId

One HTTP/runtime request:

REQ-001
analysisId

One business investigation:

ANL-001

Potentially:

REQ-001
   ↓
ANL-001
   ↓
M1 → M2 → M3 → M4 → M5

If the same analysis is viewed again:

REQ-002
   ↓
ANL-001

This is particularly important for M6 auditability.

9. Phase 3 — Request Validation

Every API should validate inputs before executing expensive logic.

For example:

metric
period
region
product
persona
question

Reject:

missing metric
invalid period
unknown KPI
invalid region
oversized request
malformed JSON

with a consistent response.

Example:

{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_REQUEST",
    "message": "Unknown KPI: revenue_growth",
    "requestId": "REQ-001"
  }
}
10. Central Error Handling

Do not let every API implement its own:

try/catch

Create:

src/server/errors.ts

with standard error classes:

ValidationError
AuthorizationError
NotFoundError
TimeoutError
RateLimitError
DependencyError

Then one central handler converts them to HTTP responses.

11. Error Taxonomy

Use predictable codes:

INVALID_REQUEST
UNAUTHORIZED
FORBIDDEN
NOT_FOUND
TIMEOUT
RATE_LIMITED
DATABASE_ERROR
LLM_ERROR
RAG_ERROR
MODULE_ERROR
INTERNAL_ERROR

This will also make M6 telemetry much cleaner.

12. Phase 4 — Runtime Executor

This is the most important M7 abstraction.

Instead of writing:

M3 timeout
M3 retry
M3 cache

M4 timeout
M4 retry
M4 cache

M5 timeout
M5 retry
M5 cache

create:

executeWithPolicy(operation, policy)

Conceptually:

executeWithPolicy
       │
       ├── timeout
       ├── retry
       ├── cache
       ├── telemetry
       ├── fallback
       └── error handling

The latest plan explicitly recommends this abstraction.

13. Runtime Executor Flow
executeWithPolicy()
       │
       ▼
Generate request context
       │
       ▼
Check cache
       │
       ├── HIT → return
       │
       ▼
Execute operation
       │
       ▼
Timeout?
       │
       ├── YES → retry/fallback
       │
       ▼
Success
       │
       ▼
Record telemetry
       │
       ▼
Return result
14. Phase 5 — Timeout

Expensive operations need explicit timeouts.

Suggested initial values:

Operation	Prototype timeout
M1 KPI	2 sec
M2 signal	2 sec
M3 driver analysis	5 sec
M4 retrieval	5 sec
M5 LLM	15 sec
Full analysis	30 sec

These are engineering defaults, not claimed benchmark values.

15. Timeout Behavior

Example:

M5
 ↓
LLM request
 ↓
15 sec timeout
 ↓
TIMEOUT
 ↓
deterministic fallback
 ↓
M6 records fallback

Do not allow an LLM request to hang the complete application.

16. Phase 6 — Retry

Retry only transient failures.

Retry:

network timeout
temporary 5xx
connection reset
temporary provider failure

Do not retry:

invalid API key
invalid request
authorization failure
bad KPI
malformed input
17. Retry Policy

Use something like:

attempt 1
   ↓
failure
   ↓
short delay
   ↓
attempt 2
   ↓
failure
   ↓
fallback/error

For the prototype:

maxRetries = 1 or 2

Avoid aggressive retry loops.

18. Phase 7 — Cache

Start with an in-memory cache.

The current architecture explicitly recommends this rather than introducing Redis.

Cache candidates:

KPI history
KPI metadata
KPI breakdown
driver analysis
retrieval results
embeddings
M5 narrative

But cache only things where stale data is acceptable or the cache key includes the relevant data/version context.

19. Cache Key Design

Bad:

revenue

Better:

kpi:revenue:2026-08:North

For intelligence:

analysis:revenue:2026-08:North:persona=executive

For embeddings:

embedding:<hash(text + model)>

The existing plan specifically recommends hash-based cache keys for embeddings.

20. Cache Invalidation

This matters for BusinessIntelligence.ai because data changes.

Cache should be invalidated when:

dataset version changes
KPI definition changes
source refreshes
embedding model changes
prompt version changes

For example:

sales_dataset:v3

and:

sales_dataset:v4

must not share stale analysis results.

21. Phase 8 — Rate Limiting

Implement basic in-memory rate limiting.

For example:

user/IP
 ↓
request counter
 ↓
window
 ↓
allow/deny

Example prototype policy:

30 requests / minute / user

The exact number can be tuned after testing.

22. Rate Limit Response

Return:

429 Too Many Requests

with:

{
  "error": {
    "code": "RATE_LIMITED",
    "message": "Too many requests",
    "requestId": "REQ-104"
  }
}
23. Phase 9 — LLM Fallback

This is critical for M5.

Normal:

M5
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
Narrative + Recommendation

Failure:

M5
 ↓
LLM
 ✗
 ↓
Deterministic fallback
 ↓
Structured narrative

The current architecture explicitly requires LLM fallback and graceful degradation.

24. What the Fallback Should Do

Do not invent a second AI system.

Use the already available deterministic information:

KPI movement
driver
contribution
evidence
confidence
recommended lever

Template:

Revenue declined by {changePct}.
The strongest identified driver was {driver},
accounting for approximately {contribution}% of the movement.
Evidence confidence is {confidence}%.

Then:

Recommendation:
{lever}
25. M5 Failure States

M5 should distinguish:

LLM_SUCCESS
LLM_TIMEOUT
LLM_ERROR
LLM_UNAVAILABLE
FALLBACK_USED

M7 executes the fallback.

M6 records it.

26. Phase 10 — RAG Fallback

If M4 fails:

M4
 ↓
RAG
 ✗

Don't fabricate evidence.

Instead:

Evidence unavailable

and M5 should be allowed to:

abstain

or provide a clearly limited structured output.

This is particularly important because the current M4 design explicitly says that insufficient evidence must not become an invented explanation.

27. Graceful Degradation Matrix
Failure	Behavior
M1 unavailable	Stop analysis
M2 unavailable	Cannot establish signal
M3 unavailable	Explain signal but no driver
M4 unavailable	Abstain / evidence unavailable
M5 LLM unavailable	Deterministic narrative
M6 unavailable	Intelligence may continue, telemetry can degrade
Database unavailable	Return dependency error
External API unavailable	Retry → fallback

This creates a credible enterprise architecture.

28. Phase 11 — Health Checks

Create:

/api/health
/api/health/live
/api/health/ready

The current plan explicitly defines these endpoints.

29. /api/health/live

Only asks:

Is the application process alive?

Response:

{
  "status": "healthy"
}

No expensive dependency checks.

30. /api/health/ready

Asks:

Can this instance actually serve requests?

Check:

database
configuration
required services

Response:

{
  "status": "healthy",
  "database": "healthy"
}

or:

{
  "status": "degraded",
  "database": "unavailable"
}
31. /api/health

Higher-level human-readable status:

TRACE SYSTEM HEALTH

Overall: DEGRADED

Database       ✓
M1             ✓
M2             ✓
M3             ✓
M4             ✓
M5 LLM         ✗
Fallback       ✓
M6             ✓
32. Phase 12 — Configuration Management

Create:

src/server/config/
├── index.ts
└── validation.ts

The current plan explicitly recommends this structure.

Centralize:

LLM provider
LLM model
timeouts
retry count
cache TTL
rate limits
database path
embedding model
feature flags
33. Environment Validation

At application startup verify required variables.

For example:

LLM_API_KEY
DATABASE_URL

if required by the chosen deployment.

Never let the application fail mysteriously later because an environment variable is missing.

34. Secrets

Never expose:

API keys
database passwords
tokens

to:

React
client bundle
audit logs
telemetry
error responses

The current M6/M7 architecture assumes server-side secret handling.

35. Phase 13 — Operational Logging

Create:

src/server/observability/logger.ts

Use structured logs:

{
  "timestamp": "...",
  "level": "INFO",
  "requestId": "REQ-001",
  "module": "M4",
  "event": "RETRIEVAL_COMPLETED",
  "durationMs": 620
}

Do not log huge business datasets.

36. Logging Levels

Use:

DEBUG
INFO
WARN
ERROR

Example:

INFO
M3 completed

WARN
M4 evidence confidence low

ERROR
LLM provider unavailable
37. Phase 14 — Telemetry

M7 should produce:

request latency
module latency
errors
retry count
fallback count
cache hits
cache misses
rate-limit events
LLM calls

The latest plan explicitly identifies these as M7 telemetry outputs.

38. Runtime Telemetry Example
{
  "requestId": "REQ-001",
  "analysisId": "ANL-001",
  "totalLatencyMs": 1240,
  "modules": {
    "M1": 42,
    "M2": 51,
    "M3": 124,
    "M4": 620,
    "M5": 403
  },
  "retries": 1,
  "cacheHits": 2,
  "fallbackUsed": false
}

M6 can then consume this.

expected refresh

staleness

43. Should You Build the Scheduler Now?

Not initially.

For the Round 2 prototype:

Manual ingestion
+

+
working governance
+
working runtime

is more valuable than spending hours building a production scheduler.

Implement scheduler only after the interactive flow works.

49. Phase 23 — Cost Protection

Before calling an LLM:

estimate request
 ↓
check token limit
 ↓
check budget
 ↓
execute

If the request is excessive:

LLM
 ↓
blocked/degraded
 ↓
deterministic fallback

This supports the challenge's explicit requirement to consider LLM economics.

Do it only after reliability is stable.

55. Why P95 Matters

Average:

1.2 sec

can hide:

1.2
1.1
1.3
1.0
9.8

P95 exposes tail latency.

For enterprise systems, this is much more meaningful.

64. NOW: What Is Still Remaining for the Whole BusinessIntelligence.ai?

This is the more important part of your question.

The Round 2 brief requires eight core capabilities:

Material KPI detection
Heterogeneous data reconciliation
Driver ranking
Evidence-backed narratives
Uncertainty/abstention
Action recommendations
Feedback learning
Security/cost/latency/scalability

It also specifically requires 3–5 connected KPIs across 2–3 sources, a semantic contract, two personas, a multi-factor KPI movement, low-confidence case, sparse-history case, role-based security, evidence freshness/method/contribution/confidence/lineage, LLM vs non-LLM transparency, and runtime telemetry.

So your remaining work should be treated as product completion, not merely M7 completion.

Demonstrate:

North Regional Manager

tries:

South data

Expected:

ACCESS DENIED

and:

M6 audit event

This satisfies the role-based entitlement requirement.

80. Required Edge Case #4 — LLM Failure

 ↓

This is a very strong demo.

confidence reduced

request clarification

This demonstrates real analytical uncertainty.

Bottom line

   ↓

   ↓

The strongest remaining implementation gap for BusinessIntelligence.ai as a whole is not Kubernetes, distributed infrastructure, or more AI. It is making the existing analytical modules behave as one governed, evidence-backed, uncertainty-aware, user-data-capable intelligence-to-action product, and then using M6/M7 to prove that the result is trustworthy and operationally reliable.



















Overall System Design & 3-Day Implementation Strategy — TRACE / BusinessIntelligence.ai

Based on the latest implementation plan and the official Accenture Innovation Challenge Round 2 brief, the correct strategy for the remaining 3 days is not to make all seven modules production-complete. The objective should be to make TRACE a coherent, demonstrable, end-to-end intelligence-to-action system, while implementing only the highest-value parts of M6 and M7 that prove trust, reliability, and operational readiness.

The Round 2 brief explicitly asks for a working prototype, not a production-grade enterprise platform. It requires 3–5 connected KPIs across 2–3 sources, a lightweight KPI/semantic contract, two personas, a multi-factor movement, low-confidence and sparse-history scenarios, role-based security, evidence freshness/method/contribution/confidence/lineage, LLM vs non-LLM transparency, and runtime telemetry.

1. Executive System Design
TRACE: From Signal to Decision

The fundamental architecture should be:

                         BUSINESS USER
                              │
                              ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │   DATA ONBOARDING │
                    │ CSV / XLSX / JSON │
                    └─────────┬─────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │       M1          │
                    │ DATA + SEMANTICS  │
                    │                   │
                    │ • Profiling       │
                    │ • Quality         │
                    │ • Normalization   │
                    │ • KPI definitions │
                    │ • KPI calculation │
                    └─────────┬─────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │       M2          │
                    │  SIGNAL ENGINE    │
                    │                   │

                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │       M3          │
                    │ DRIVER ANALYSIS   │
                    │                   │
                    │ • Contribution    │
                    │ • Correlation     │
                    │ • Hypotheses      │
                    │ • Ranking         │
                    └─────────┬─────────┘
                              │

                    │ EVIDENCE ENGINE   │
                    │                   │

                    │ • RAG             │

                    │ • Contradiction   │

                    │ • Evidence graph  │
                    └─────────┬─────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │       M5          │
                    │ INTELLIGENCE      │
                    │ + DECISION        │
                    │                   │
                    │ • Narrative       │
                    │ • Personas        │
                    │ • Recommendation  │
                    │ • Confidence      │
                    │ • Decision        │
                    └─────────┬─────────┘
                              │

                       HUMAN DECISION
                     ┌────────┼────────┐
                     ▼        ▼        ▼
                  ACCEPT    REJECT   CHALLENGE
                     │        │        │
                     └────────┼────────┘
                              ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐
                    │       M6          │
                    │ TRUST + GOVERNANCE │
                    │                   │
                    │ • Audit           │
                    │ • Security        │
                    │ • Evaluation      │
                    │ • Feedback        │
                    │ • Cost            │
                    │ • Drift           │
                    │ • Lineage         │
                    └───────────────────┘

       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
       │                    M7                        │
       │       RUNTIME + RELIABILITY + OPERATIONS     │
       │                                              │
       │ Validation • Timeout • Retry • Cache         │
       │ Rate limit • Fallback • Health • Telemetry   │
       │ Deployment                                   │
       └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This reflects the intended architecture: M1–M5 perform intelligence, M6 observes/governs it, and M7 operates it reliably. M6 should not merely sit after M5 because it needs information from every module, while M7 is cross-cutting runtime infrastructure.

2. The Core Design Principle

The most important architectural principle is:

The LLM is not the source of quantitative truth.

The chain of truth should be:

M1 → KPI truth
 ↓
M2 → Signal truth
 ↓
M3 → Hypothesis truth
 ↓
M4 → Evidence truth
 ↓
M5 → Narrative + Decision
 ↓
M6 → Governance

The LLM is used primarily in M5 for language synthesis and reasoning over already-verified context. M4 may optionally use AI assistance for evidence classification, but evidence provenance and quantitative truth remain controlled by deterministic/analytical components.

Therefore:

SQL
   = quantitative truth

Statistics
   = signal truth

Analytical methods
   = driver hypotheses

Retrieval
   = evidence

LLM
   = synthesis / contextual reasoning

This distinction directly addresses the Round 2 requirement to explain where SQL, business rules, statistics, retrieval and LLMs are used and why.

3. Two Data Planes

TRACE should explicitly distinguish structured business data from unstructured evidence.

A. Structured business data

Examples:

sales.csv
orders.xlsx
inventory.csv
marketing.csv
ERP
CRM
finance database

Pipeline:

Upload / Connect
       ↓
Schema inference
       ↓
Data profiling
       ↓
Column mapping
       ↓
Validation
       ↓
Normalization
       ↓
Governed dataset
       ↓
M1
       ↓
M2
       ↓
M3
B. Unstructured contextual evidence

Examples:

PDF
DOCX
TXT
support tickets
operations reports
management reports
customer complaints

Pipeline:

Document
   ↓
Text extraction
   ↓
Metadata
   ↓
Chunking
   ↓
Embedding
   ↓
Retrieval
   ↓
Evidence scoring
   ↓
M4

The implementation plan explicitly establishes these as two different data flows.

4. M1 — Data Foundation & Semantic Layer
Responsibility

M1 answers:

"What is the number, and what exactly does it mean?"

It owns:

data ingestion
profiling
data quality
transformation
KPI definitions
KPI calculation
KPI history
lineage metadata

The intended semantic contract is approximately:

KPI: Revenue

Formula:
SUM(revenue)

Dimensions:
region
product
channel
date

Drivers:
orders
AOV
discount
stockouts

Threshold:
5%

Owner:
Sales

This directly maps to the Round 2 requirement for a lightweight semantic/KPI contract.

Three-day scope

Implement only:

CSV
XLSX
JSON

for user onboarding.

Do not attempt real ERP/CRM connectors in three days.

Remaining
[ ] Generic uploaded-data pipeline
[ ] Schema detection
[ ] Column mapping
[ ] Dataset ID/version
[ ] User confirmation
[ ] Explicit semantic contract UI
[ ] Freshness metadata
[ ] Source lineage

The existing M1 foundation is useful, but the user-facing onboarding layer needs to be made explicit.

5. M2 — KPI Signal Engine
Responsibility

M2 answers:

"Is this movement actually meaningful?"

It should determine:

Current value
Previous value
% change
Historical baseline
Statistical significance
Business materiality
Confidence
Priority

For example:

Revenue

Current:
₹760.5M

Change:
-17.77%

Z-score:
-3.81

Materiality:
HIGH

Signal:
INVESTIGATE
Existing state

The implementation plan considers the core M2/M3 analytical foundation sufficiently developed for the hackathon, with the emphasis now on verification, hardening and integration rather than rewriting the mathematical architecture.

Three-day priority

Do not redesign M2.

Only verify:

[ ] Materiality
[ ] Confidence
[ ] Sparse-history handling
[ ] Missing-data handling
[ ] Correct API contract
[ ] M1 → M2 integration
[ ] Deterministic tests
6. M3 — Driver & Hypothesis Engine
Responsibility

M3 answers:

"What could explain the movement?"

Example:

Revenue ↓ 17.77%

Driver ranking:

1. Orders
   Contribution: 81.6%
   Confidence: High

2. AOV
   Contribution: 13.2%
   Confidence: Medium

3. Price
   Evidence: Insufficient

It should produce candidate explanations, not causal certainty.

That distinction is important.

Correlation / contribution
          ≠
Causation
Current status

M3 is considered complete enough for the hackathon, but it should not be treated as universally complete simply because its tests pass. The remaining work is primarily verification, hardening, uploaded-data generalization and the M3→M4 contract.

Three-day priority
[ ] Freeze M3
[ ] Verify output schema
[ ] Connect M2 → M3
[ ] Connect M3 → M4
[ ] Test sparse history
[ ] Test missing driver

Do not spend a full day rewriting M3.

7. M4 — Evidence & RAG Engine

This is one of the most important remaining technical components.

Responsibility

M4 answers:

"What evidence supports or contradicts the candidate explanation?"

Current intended flow:

M3 Hypotheses
      ↓
Query Builder
      ↓
Structured Evidence
      +
Unstructured Evidence
      ↓
Retrieval
      ↓
Ranking
      ↓
Evidence Classification
      ↓
SUPPORT / CONTRADICT / NEUTRAL
      ↓
Evidence Confidence
      ↓
Provenance
      ↓
Evidence Graph

The latest plan identifies the current M4 implementation as a placeholder shell, with random-vector similarity rather than real semantic retrieval, and a simplified graph rather than actual hypothesis/evidence/source relationships.

Therefore M4 is a critical remaining gap.

Three-day target:

[ ] Replace random embeddings
[ ] Real embedding generation
[ ] Document chunking
[ ] Metadata
[ ] Semantic retrieval
[ ] Top-k ranking
[ ] Support/contradict/neutral
[ ] Evidence confidence
[ ] Source citation
[ ] Provenance
[ ] Basic evidence graph

Do not build a sophisticated vector database.

For the prototype, a small local/persisted retrieval implementation is sufficient.

8. M5 — Intelligence-to-Action
Responsibility

M5 answers:

"What is the defensible story, and what should we do?"

M5 receives:

M1 KPI truth
+
M2 signal truth
+
M3 driver hypotheses
+
M4 EvidencePackage
+
User persona
+
Business context

The plan explicitly defines an IntelligenceContext to combine these inputs.

M5 output

The output should contain:

What happened?
Why?
Evidence
Confidence
Uncertainty
Recommendation
Owner
Expected impact
Monitoring metric

Recommendation structure:

Driver
   ↓
Controllable lever
   ↓
Action
   ↓
Expected impact
   ↓
Owner
   ↓
Confidence
   ↓
Monitoring plan

This is directly aligned with the challenge's recommended action structure.

9. M5 Personas

The challenge requires at least two personas.

Use only:

Executive
Analyst

for the prototype.

The implementation plan explicitly recommends starting with these two rather than attempting six personas.

Executive
Revenue declined 17.77%, primarily associated
with an 81.6% contribution from order-volume decline.

Business impact:
HIGH

Recommended priority:
Recover order volume in affected regions.

Confidence:
86%
Analyst
Revenue:
-17.77%

Primary candidate:
Orders

Contribution:
81.6%

Secondary:
AOV

Evidence:
4 supporting
2 contradictory
1 neutral

Confidence:
86%

Next analysis:
Investigate regional order conversion.

Same underlying truth.

Different decision context.

10. M5 LLM Strategy

The correct order is:

M1
 ↓
M2
 ↓
M3
 ↓
M4
 ↓
Deterministic context
 ↓
Prompt Builder
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
Structured Output
 ↓
Schema validation
 ↓
Grounding checks
 ↓
Final narrative

The LLM must not receive unrestricted database access.

The current plan explicitly recommends adding the LLM only after the deterministic story structure works.

Three-day decision

If the deterministic M5 works:

then add LLM.

If the LLM integration becomes unstable:

keep the deterministic narrative as the fallback.

Do not allow an LLM integration problem to break the entire demo.

11. M6 — Trust & Governance Layer

M6 should operate across M1–M5, not simply after M5.

                    M6
       ┌──────────────────────────┐
       │ Security                 │
       │ Audit                    │
       │ Lineage                  │
       │ Evaluation               │
       │ Telemetry                │
       │ Cost                     │
       │ Drift                    │
       │ Feedback                 │
       │ Governance               │
       └──────────────────────────┘
          ↓    ↓    ↓    ↓    ↓
         M1   M2   M3   M4   M5

This is the intended architecture.

12. The Most Important M6 Feature: analysisId

Every investigation receives:

ANL-001

Then:

ANL-001
│
├── M1
├── M2
├── M3
├── M4
├── M5
└── HUMAN DECISION

For example:

ANL-001

M1:
Revenue = ₹760.5M

M2:
Change = -17.77%
Signal = INVESTIGATE

M3:
Orders = 81.6% contribution

M4:
7 evidence items
4 support
2 contradict
1 neutral

M5:
Recommendation generated

Human:
ACCEPTED

This gives TRACE a genuine end-to-end traceability mechanism.

The implementation plan explicitly identifies analysisId as the first M6 implementation because subsequent governance capabilities depend on it.

16. M6 Feedback Loop

      ↓

But automated model learning is not required in three days. The challenge asks for a mechanism to learn from feedback, not necessarily online model retraining.

17. M7 — Runtime & Reliability Layer

M7 should not become a massive infrastructure project.

Its job is:

Can TRACE operate reliably?

It wraps M1–M6 with:

Request ID
Validation
Error handling
Timeout
Retry
Cache
Rate limiting
Fallback
Health
Telemetry
Deployment

The latest plan explicitly marks these as designed but largely pending implementation.

18. M7 Runtime Executor

The central abstraction should be:

executeWithPolicy()

instead of implementing timeout/retry separately inside every module.

executeWithPolicy()
       │
       ├── request ID
       ├── validation
       ├── cache
       ├── timeout
       ├── retry
       ├── fallback
       ├── telemetry
       └── error handling

Then:

M1
M2
M3
M4
M5

all use the same runtime mechanism.

22. One Unified Analysis Object

The strongest architectural improvement in these final three days is to make every module communicate through structured contracts.

This means the frontend doesn't need to know:

✓ Driver contribution calculated

✓ Contradictory evidence checked
✓ Confidence calibrated
✓ Narrative grounded

Processing:

M1 → Deterministic
M2 → Statistical

M4 → Retrieval
M5 → LLM-assisted

This directly addresses the challenge's requirement for transparency between LLM and non-LLM processing.

29. Required Scenario 2 — Low Confidence

The challenge explicitly requires this.

New KPI
+
limited history
+
conflicting evidence

LOW CONFIDENCE

M3:
Insufficient historical support

M4:
Conflicting evidence

M5:
"I cannot establish a reliable explanation
from the available evidence."

This is much more impressive than forcing an AI answer.

33. Required Scenario 6 — Contradictory Evidence

Inventory increased 20%
→ CONTRADICT

Mixed evidence

M5:

Confidence reduced

Alternative explanation required

Potentially:

TRACE cannot confidently establish
stockout as the sole cause.

This directly demonstrates the challenge's requirement for uncertainty and contradictory evidence.

40. What "Complete" Means After 3 Days

Do not define completion as:

"All seven modules are production-ready."

That is impossible and unnecessary.

Define completion as:

TRACE can take business data, identify a meaningful KPI movement, explain candidate drivers, retrieve and challenge evidence, generate persona-specific actionable insight, communicate uncertainty, capture a human decision, maintain an audit trail, enforce basic access control, and continue operating through controlled AI/evidence failures.

That is a much stronger and more defensible definition.

This is the architecture I would freeze now. The implementation plan already establishes the distinction between intelligence, governance and production layers, and explicitly advises designing with M7 in mind without attempting enterprise-scale infrastructure during the prototype.

43. Conclusion
TRACE is not another LLM-powered BI dashboard

Traditional BI answers:

What happened?

TRACE is designed to answer:

What happened → Why did it happen → What evidence supports that explanation → How confident are we → What should we do → Did the human accept it?

The architecture deliberately separates these responsibilities.

M1 establishes governed quantitative truth.

M2 determines whether a movement is materially and statistically meaningful.

M3 identifies and ranks candidate drivers without pretending correlation is causation.

M4 challenges those hypotheses against structured and unstructured evidence, explicitly surfacing supporting, contradictory and missing evidence.

M5 converts verified analytical context into persona-specific narratives and actionable recommendations while allowing abstention when the evidence is insufficient.

M6 makes the system trustworthy through security, auditability, lineage, evaluation, feedback, cost and quality monitoring.

M7 makes the system operationally reliable through runtime controls, timeout, retry, fallback, health monitoring and telemetry.

This architecture directly reflects the challenge's core objective of building a KPI intelligence-to-action engine, rather than simply adding an LLM to an existing dashboard.

With only three days remaining, the winning strategy is therefore:
        DO NOT BUILD EVERYTHING
                  │
                  ▼
       BUILD ONE COMPLETE PATH
                  │
                  ▼
      M1 → M2 → M3 → M4 → M5
                  │
                  ▼
            HUMAN DECISION
                  │
                  ▼
                 M6
                  ▲
                  │
                 M7
                  │
                  ▼
          RELIABLE OPERATION

The final prototype should be deep rather than broad: one excellent end-to-end investigation, two personas, real evidence, explicit uncertainty, one security boundary, one human feedback/decision loop, and controlled failure demonstrations.

That is achievable in three days and, more importantly, gives the judges a clear answer to the central question:

Why should a business trust TRACE to move from a KPI signal to a real decision?

Because TRACE does not ask the LLM to invent the answer. It builds the answer progressively from governed data, statistical signals, analytical hypotheses, traceable evidence, explicit uncertainty, human judgment and operational controls.
